#2951. 5 Ways to Reset & Reach Your Fitness Goals

Life happens. Maybe you’ve been crushing your workouts and suddenly hit a wall. Or maybe you’re just coming out of a busy season where your workouts took a back seat.

Wherever you’re at right now, it’s okay. The key to long-term success isn’t perfection, it’s the ability to reset, refocus, and keep going.

If you’re ready to recommit to your goals and make real progress, here’s exactly how to reset and reach your fitness goals. Don't wait to till tomorrow, start today.

1. Reconnect with Your WHY

Before jumping back into workouts or overhauling your routine, take a moment to reconnect with why you are on this journey. Do you want to feel more confident in your skin? Boost your energy? Build strength and endurance? Lose weight? Feel better from the inside out?

Write it down. Keep it somewhere you’ll see daily, whether it’s your journal, your bathroom mirror, or your vision board. 

2. Start Fresh with a Plan

The fastest way to kill momentum is trying to “wing it.” That’s why having a structured plan makes ALL the difference. And we took all the guess work out for you and have a plan made, all you need to do is follow it and stay consistent. 

Join our Summer Shape Up Challenge and get all the details, here!

SSU is our 6-week challenge designed to help you burn fat, build lean muscle, and get in your best shape, physically and mentally - before summer ends. It kicks off July 7th! 

All the details:

  • A full training plan in our MOVE app
  • Summer meal plan to fuel your goals
  • Weekly check-ins from me
  • Weekly Giveaways
  • A shot at the Grand Prize ($2000 value)
  • Snatched summer glow-up

Whether you’ve fallen off or you’re starting fresh, this is your built-in reset button.

3. Clean Up Your Environment

Your space impacts your energy and habits. Do a quick reset by:

  • Clean up your kitchen: Ditch the expired and processed snacks and restock with fresh, whole foods

  • Lay out your workout clothes the night before

  • Set up your workout space in your favorite spot the night before so you are ready for your quick morning movement

4. Track Small Wins

It’s easy to focus on how far you have to go, but the real habits start when you celebrate the little wins along the way.

  • You got in a 10-minute workout? Win.

  • You made a protein smoothie instead of skipping breakfast? Win.

  • You did your MOVE app workout 3 days in a row? HUGE win.

5. Accountability

Trying to reach your fitness goals alone can feel isolating and you can lose momentum fast. Joining an Accountability Group or finding an accountability partner to do check ins with, chat about your wins and your lows, can make all the difference in how you show up every single day.

This is your chance to recommit to YOU.

Join our Summer Shape Up Challenge and start with us on July 7th!

#2952. Philippines Digital Nomad Visa 2026: What EO 86 Says, and Why You Can’t Apply Yet

If you have a foreign partner, a client abroad or an expat friend, you have probably been asked this already. They read that the Philippines now has a digital nomad visa, they picture a month in Siargao with a laptop, and they want to know where to apply.

The honest answer, as of 11 October 2026: nowhere, yet. The law exists. The application form doesn’t.

The Philippines digital nomad visa was created by Executive Order No. 86, signed on 24 April 2025. Nearly eighteen months later, most guides online still contradict themselves, calling the visa “fully operational” in one paragraph and admitting the fee and income rule are unknown in the next. This guide is for Filipinos who keep getting asked about it, for Fil-Ams and former Filipinos thinking of working from home for a year, and for foreign partners and friends planning a long stay here. We go through what the order actually says, line by line, what is still missing, who can realistically qualify, and what people do instead while the Department of Foreign Affairs (DFA) finishes the job.

A few links in this guide are affiliate links. If you buy through them we may earn a small commission at no extra cost to you, and we only link what we would use ourselves.

This is general information, not legal or immigration advice. Rules change, so confirm your own case with the DFA, the Bureau of Immigration or a licensed immigration lawyer before you act on it.

Here is where things stand as of 11 October 2026, checked against government sources on that date.

If you only read one line: the Philippines has authorized a digital nomad visa, but as of this check there is no public way to apply for it.

The order is short. Section 1 lets the DFA issue a Digital Nomad Visa (DNV) to non-immigrant foreigners who want to enter and stay temporarily “for the purpose of working remotely using digital technologies”, where their clients or employers are outside the Philippines. Section 2 lists eight conditions. The DFA’s own embassy in The Hague publishes the same eight conditions on its DNV page, word for word, which is the closest thing to an official checklist right now.

Section 3 says a DNV holder may stay up to one year and may renew for the same duration. Section 4 tells the DFA to set the grounds for revoking a DNV, and says those grounds must include, among others, taking a local job, fraud or misrepresentation in the application, and breaking immigration law. Section 5 puts the DFA in charge, working with the Department of Justice, the Department of Tourism, the Bureau of Immigration and the Bureau of Internal Revenue, and orders a pilot within 60 days. Section 7 orders implementing guidelines within 30 days.

That is it. No fee, no income figure, no form, no processing time.

Search “Philippines digital nomad visa requirements” and you will see USD 24,000 a year everywhere. It is not in EO 86. Trace it back and it leads to consultancies and nomad websites, never to a DFA notice. At the BSP reference rate of PHP 62.90 to the dollar (9 October 2026), that is about PHP 1.51 million a year, or PHP 125,800 a month, and nobody official has said it.

None of this means those figures will turn out wrong. It means nobody can quote them as the rule yet. When the guidelines do appear, the income figure and the fee will be the first things to check, and we will update this post with the DFA’s own numbers.

If someone offers to “process” a Philippine DNV for a fee today, ask them which DFA circular they are filing under. There isn’t one we can find.

This is the part almost every guide skips, and it is the most Filipino part of the whole order.

Section 2(f) says the applicant “must be a national of a country that offers DNVs to Filipinos”. So whether a foreigner can get the Philippine nomad visa depends on how their own country treats Philippine passport holders. The DFA has not published a list. But we already track which nomad visas Filipinos can actually get, so the mirror image is easy to sketch.

Countries whose nomad or remote-work visas were open to Philippine passports in our September 2026 check: Thailand (DTV), South Korea, Spain, Portugal, Hungary, Croatia, Estonia, Malta, the UAE, Malaysia (DE Rantau), Indonesia and Colombia. Taiwan’s Gold Card is also open to Filipinos, but it is an open work permit rather than a nomad visa, so we leave it off. Our full breakdown, with income rules in pesos, is in our guide to the digital nomad visas Filipinos can actually get.

Countries that, on a strict reading, fail the test:

So the people most likely to ask you about it, an American boyfriend or an Australian boss, are the ones the order shuts out. Whether the DFA reads Section 2(f) that strictly is exactly what the missing guidelines should settle. Until then, treat this list as our reading, not an official one.

The Foreign Service Post part is softer than it looks. The last paragraph of Section 2 says a national of a qualifying country with no Philippine embassy or consulate may apply in the nearest country that has one, subject to the guidelines.

The deadlines in the order passed long ago. EO 86 takes effect on publication, and gave 30 days for guidelines and 60 days for a pilot. Both windows closed in mid-2025.

Here is what we checked on 11 October 2026:

Other reports disagree. A July 2026 column in the Daily Tribune said the DFA “began accepting applications” after the order took effect. Nomad visa trackers that checked in September 2026 found no working channel. We could not confirm either way from a primary source, so here is the practical test: email the Philippine embassy or consulate that covers your country and ask, in writing, whether it is accepting DNV applications and on which form. A written yes from a Foreign Service Post beats any blog, including this one.

Our first look at this visa was written before the order was even signed, and our earlier guide shows how much has stayed the same since.

Without a DNV, long-staying foreigners use the routes that already work.

The visa-free entry plus extensions route. Nationals of visa-free countries get an initial 30 days on arrival, according to the Bureau of Immigration. They can extend at any BI office or through BI e-services online. DFA posts describing the BI’s Long-Stay Visitor Visa Extension say a stay can be extended in blocks of up to six months, to a maximum of 36 months in total for visa-free nationals and 24 months for nationals who need a visa. Stays past 59 days also need an ACR I-Card, which we explain in our guide to getting an ACR I-Card in the Philippines.

One honest caveat. A tourist stay is not a work status, and we could not find any BI guidance saying remote work for a foreign employer is fine on one. Plenty of foreigners do it quietly. The DNV was created to close that gray area, which is why its delay matters.

The balikbayan route, for Fil-Ams, OFWs and former Filipinos. Under Republic Act 6768 as amended by RA 9174, a balikbayan is a Filipino citizen who has lived abroad continuously for at least a year, an OFW, or a former Filipino naturalized abroad. A former Filipino gets a one-year visa-free stay on arrival, unless they hold the passport of a restricted nationality. The foreign spouse and children of any balikbayan get the same one-year stay, as long as they travel together with the balikbayan. Bring an old Philippine passport or PSA birth certificate, plus the marriage or birth certificate for family members. For most of our US readers, this already beats the DNV: one year, no application, no fee. We cover the details in our guide to the one-year visa-free balikbayan stay for foreign spouses and children.

Dual citizenship. A former Filipino who reacquires citizenship under RA 9225 is simply Filipino again, with no stay limit and no visa question at all.

Since the DNV has no published fee, the real numbers today belong to the tourist and balikbayan routes. Conversions use the BSP reference rate of PHP 62.90 to USD 1 on 9 October 2026.

Health cover is the one cost that is certain either way. The DNV requires insurance valid for the whole stay, and a long tourist stay without it is a gamble with Philippine hospital deposits. SafetyWing’s Nomad Insurance is one option, because it renews month to month.

See what SafetyWing Nomad Insurance costs for your stay

Long-stay foreigners tend to split time between a city with reliable fiber and a beach town. Cebu IT Park and BGC in Taguig have the coworking spaces and backup power. Siargao and La Union have the surf and the community, with more brownouts and slower internet. Budget for a backup connection anywhere outside Metro Manila and Metro Cebu, and remember that typhoon season runs roughly June to November.

For La Union, San Juan is the base, with surf camps and coworking cafes along the national highway. You can compare places to stay in San Juan, La Union before you commit to a month.

For Siargao, most remote workers stay around General Luna, close to Cloud 9. The map below shows current places to stay there, with prices in pesos.

A local SIM from Globe or Smart is cheap once you are here, but an eSIM means you land already connected for the airport transfer and the first client call. Check Airalo’s Philippines eSIM plans before you fly.

Is the Philippines digital nomad visa open for applications? Not that we can confirm. As of 11 October 2026 it is not on the DFA e-Visa portal, and we found no published implementing guidelines.

How much income do you need for the Philippines digital nomad visa? EO 86 says only “sufficient income” earned outside the Philippines. The widely quoted USD 24,000 a year is unofficial.

How long is the Philippines digital nomad visa valid? Up to one year, renewable for the same duration, under Section 3 of EO 86.

Can Filipinos apply for the Philippines digital nomad visa? No, and they don’t need to. It is for foreign nationals. Filipino citizens and dual citizens live and work in the Philippines without a visa.

Can Americans get the Philippines digital nomad visa? On a strict reading, no, because the US offers no digital nomad visa to Filipinos. The DFA has not confirmed how it will apply the rule.

Can a digital nomad visa holder work for a Philippine company? No. Section 4 says the grounds for revocation must include local employment.

My foreign husband works remotely. What should he use? If you have lived abroad continuously for at least a year, are an OFW, or are a former Filipino, he can enter together with you under the balikbayan privilege for a one-year visa-free stay, as long as he is not a restricted national. If you live in the Philippines already, the balikbayan rule does not apply, so visa-free entry plus BI extensions is the usual route.

The Philippines digital nomad visa is real on paper and missing in practice. Until the DFA publishes guidelines, a form and a fee, the useful routes are the old ones: visa-free entry with extensions, the balikbayan privilege for Filipinos abroad, former Filipinos and their families, or dual citizenship. If a foreign friend asks you about it this week, send them this post and tell them to email their nearest Philippine embassy for a written answer. We will update this guide with the official figures as soon as the DFA releases them.

Save this guide for later: pin one of these to your Pinterest board.

Bianca Rei is a Filipino traveler, entrepreneur, and problem-solver who has called Serbia home for many years. She has been following Two Monkeys Travel Group since 2014, back when she was simply a reader dreaming, traveling, and collecting stories of her own, so being able to contribute to the blog now feels a little like coming full circle.

Bianca runs Rodi Solutions, where she helps people and businesses navigate life and business in Serbia, alongside website design, development, and management. When she is not working, she is usually traveling, boxing, chasing another adventure, or spending time with her family.

#2953. A rock plunged 90 kilometers into Earth and revealed something unexpected

A remarkable rock discovered in Papua New Guinea has revealed an unexpected temperature pattern deep beneath Earth's surface, challenging scientists' understanding of how tectonic plates move into the planet's interior.

An international research team led by Göttingen University found that the rock traveled more than 90 kilometers (56 miles) underground about four million years ago. Along the way, it experienced unusually high temperatures at shallower depths before entering conditions associated with a relatively cooler type of subduction.

The discovery raises new questions about how heat is distributed inside Earth and how tectonic plates behave as they descend. The findings were published in Nature Geoscience.

A Strange Discovery Deep Beneath Earth's Surface

Earth's outer shell is divided into enormous tectonic plates that slowly move across the planet. When two plates converge, one can be forced beneath the other and sink into Earth's interior. This process is called "subduction."

These regions play a major role in shaping our planet. They are associated with powerful earthquakes and volcanic activity, but they also help regulate Earth's climate over millions of years by recycling materials such as carbon dioxide through the planet's interior.

The immense pressure found in subduction zones can transform rocks into a distinctive red and green material known as "eclogite." Researchers examined one of these rocks from Papua New Guinea using several advanced analytical techniques.

Inside the rock, they discovered tiny crystals of coesite, a mineral that can form only under extremely high pressure. These crystals had become trapped within another mineral as the rock formed, preserving valuable evidence of the conditions it experienced deep underground.

By studying these microscopic clues, the scientists determined that the rock had descended at least 90 kilometers beneath Earth's surface, where temperatures reached approximately 800°C (1,472°F).

Even more surprising was what they uncovered about its earlier journey.

An Unexpected Shift From Hot to Cold Subduction

The researchers reconstructed the rock's temperature history and found that at a depth of approximately 45 kilometers (28 miles), it had already reached temperatures of around 700°C (1,292°F).

That meant the rock was only about 100°C cooler at half its maximum depth, even though it still had another 45 kilometers to descend.

This was unexpected because existing models predict a different distribution of heat within subduction zones. The rock appears to have experienced unusually hot conditions at shallower depths before entering an environment with a relatively cooler temperature pattern farther down. Although its actual temperature increased with depth, the rate of warming was much smaller than scientists would normally expect.

Dr. Jan Schönig at Göttingen University's Geoscience Center explains: "Finding this change from hot to cold subduction was unexpected as this contradicts our current thermal models of subduction zones."

Scientists have previously discovered rocks containing evidence of unexpectedly high temperatures. However, this is the first time researchers have been able to reconstruct such a clear transition between different thermal conditions as a rock descended from shallower to much greater depths.

What Could Explain Earth's Hidden Heat Mystery?

The researchers have identified several possible explanations for the unusual temperature pattern.

One possibility is that tectonic plates generate substantially more heat at relatively shallow depths than scientists previously believed. As the plates move against each other, intense shearing forces could produce additional heating along their boundaries.

Another explanation is that the subduction zone had not yet existed long enough to develop the cooler temperatures predicted by conventional models.

Both processes may also have contributed to the rock's unusual thermal history.

Understanding these temperature differences is important because subduction zones influence some of Earth's most powerful geological processes. More accurate models could help scientists better evaluate the conditions associated with earthquakes and volcanic activity.

The findings could also improve our understanding of major geochemical cycles, particularly the long-term carbon cycle, which helps regulate Earth's climate by moving carbon between the surface and the planet's interior.

By revealing how conditions change as tectonic plates descend, the discovery offers scientists a new way to investigate how subduction has operated over millions to billions of years and how these processes continue shaping Earth today.

The research received funding from the German Research Foundation (DFG).

Materials provided by University of GÃ¶ttingen. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#2954. The Bursar

Thank you for visiting nature.com. You are using a browser version with limited support for CSS. To obtain the best experience, we recommend you use a more up to date browser (or turn off compatibility mode in Internet Explorer). In the meantime, to ensure continued support, we are displaying the site without styles and JavaScript.

Marissa Lingen has published more than 100 short stories in venues such as Analog, Lightspeed and Tor.com.

The Bursar shook Cerise’s hand with firm confidence. “We don’t expect you to be familiar with procedures,” he said. “Orientation for our new parents is standard. Don’t be embarrassed to ask questions.”

“I suppose when Lorelei is my age the parents will take it for granted.” Cerise smiled in self-deprecation.

The Bursar nodded. “Of course, we expect our system to progress a great deal more by then. Now, your daughter Lorelei — she’s five?”

“Five in June,” Cerise agreed. “A normal, happy little girl, we’d like to think.”

“Our programme will help keep her that way.” The Bursar gestured for Cerise to follow him. The halls were bright and spacious, decorated with printouts of student artwork.

“Lorelei will have her own cubicle, kindergarten through graduation,” said the Bursar, gesturing at one of the dim nooks off the bright hallway. “One advantage of our programme is decreased contagion — although that’s not its main appeal.”

Read more science fiction from Nature Futures

Read more science fiction from Nature Futures

Cerise peered in at the tiny, still form. She felt like a voyeur into this strange industrial transformation of sleep. She forced a laugh. “A far cry from showing me the gym and the art room, isn’t it?”

The Bursar pursed his lips. “Our students do experience those things. In some ways the virtual environment is superior — you and I didn’t see a new gymnasium remodel more than once, if ever. Lorelei will never experience anything but. No matter how the state of the art changes, our experience changes with it.”

They finished the tour in silence — not much to see except for the nurse’s office, which didn’t look much like the nurses’ offices of Cerise’s childhood except for its antiseptic gleam. Satisfied, the Bursar escorted her back to his office.

“We used to think that children just had to take whatever life threw at them,” he beamed, relaxing into his leather chair and leaving Cerise to perch on a wooden one. “What a terrible idea, raising children on random trauma! Then we reversed course and over-restricted them until their prefrontal cortexes developed.” He shook his head. “Lunacy. Learning requires error.”

“Of course,” said Cerise stiffly, one of that protected cohort herself.

But the Bursar was giving his favourite speech and could not be distracted. “Our calibrated experience will allow Lorelei to only experience the proper amount of error, conflict and pain. We can’t do what some of the upper-end models do, but at your budget … I think you’ll be pleased with her educational experience.”

“What about external … problems? Obviously we try not to have any,” Cerise hurried to explain. “But what if Lorelei has a grandparent with cancer, or something traumatic like that?”

“It’s crucial for you to report to us regularly. Even small trauma — she twists her ankle playing at the park — is used to customize her programme. We’d substitute a nurturing experience in for a challenge there. We’ll assess her reactions constantly, fitting her challenges to her means of addressing them, but our weekly report from parents is crucial. Just think what would happen if we were giving her scenarios like fighting with a friend at recess on top of her parents getting a divorce! Disaster.”

“We’re not getting a divorce,” Cerise blurted defensively, and then made herself add, “I know, you just meant … for example. But to take your example, can she really have fights with friends at recess? Do you connect the children’s simulations to each other?”

“Absolutely not!” snapped the Bursar. “Children are chaotic! You might as well send her to traditional school if that was all you cared for her brain development.”

“But … doesn’t that mean she won’t share childhood memories with anybody?” Cerise thought about the time Lucy Meisner threw up on a field trip to the zoo, the time she and Tony Azar got in trouble for hiding at the end of recess and how it made them laugh decades later.

“Many adults don’t remain close with their classmates from traditional schooling, either,” said the Bursar stiffly. “We feel the trade-off is well worth it.”

“I understand.” Cerise sighed. “So I expect you’ll have the fee structure to go over.”

His face closed disapprovingly. “I will direct you to that department on your way out.”

#2956. What Is a Primal Diet?

A Primal diet is not a “diet” in the way the word is commonly used. It’s not a rigid set of rules centered around caloric restriction or “allowed” foods, usually prescribed for the express purpose of weight loss. Instead, a Primal diet honors, approximates, and emulates the spirit of the dietary environment available to humans for most of our history.

To eat according to the Primal Blueprint means choosing foods that provide the body with all the building blocks it needs to function (amino acids, fatty acids, nutrients, and more) while avoiding foods, and modern “frankenfoods,” that erode your health. It means giving your body all the energy it needs to be strong, active, and well.

In answer to the hugely contentious question of which diet—plant-based, vegan, carnivore, Mediterranean, “everything in moderation”—is best, the Primal Blueprint puts forth a simple answer: The best diet for humans is one comprising the foods that humans are designed to eat.

Despite what you might have heard about Primal, paleo, and the more general ancestral health movement, the goal isn’t to get you to eat “like a caveman.” For one thing, many of the foods that were around millennia ago have been changed by natural evolution and human agriculture. Furthermore, the foods your far-back ancestors ate depended entirely on the geographic location from which they hailed.

The environments in which we eat are also different. Food has never been more abundant and easier to procure. We are more stressed, more rushed, and more sedentary. We are less exposed to dirt and the accompanying microbes that populate the gastrointestinal tract.  In short, the idea isn’t that we should be eating exactly like our ancestors did.

The Primal Blueprint takes lessons from human history and modern science to decide what, when, why, and how (much) to eat—and, importantly, what to avoid.

The best diet for humans is one comprising the foods that humans are designed to eat.

The 10 Primal Laws start with diet because of all the environmental inputs we can leverage for proper gene expression, diet arguably has the biggest impact in the shortest time.

This is the basic description of everything our ancestors ate to get the protein, fats, carbohydrates, vitamins, minerals, antioxidants, phenols, fiber, water, and other nutrients necessary to sustain life, build strong muscles, expend lots of energy each day moving about,  maintain healthy immune systems, evolve larger brains, and raise healthy children.

As you can see, this law leaves plenty of room for you to structure your diet according to your personal tastes, preferences, and needs. Perhaps you prefer to eat relatively more plants than animals, or vice versa. You might be a gourmand who takes great pleasure in creating elaborate dishes and trying new foods, or maybe you’re content to repeat a few simple meals over and over.

As long as you prioritize close-to-nature foods from these broad categories, you’re headed in the right direction.

Humans’ ability to exploit almost every corner of this earth was partly predicated on their ability to consume vastly different types of plant and animal life. Exploring a new environment and trying new foods posed a danger: the new food might contain potent toxins.

You probably don’t have to fear food-borne illness anymore, aside from an occasional bout with non-lethal food poisoning. Instead, we contend with ubiquitous modern foods that undermine our health more slowly and more insidiously. Whereas our ancestors’ keen senses of smell and taste helped sort out the good from the bad, our ability to distinguish good from bad is now thwarted by food manufacturing and clever marketing.

Understanding what to eat is only half the battle. You still have to translate that knowledge into action. Here are some other factors that come into play.

Protein takes priority. The amino acids in protein don’t just go toward making muscle. They’re necessary for all structures in the body, for making hormones and neurotransmitters, and for facilitating enzymatic reactions.

Protein is also highly satiating, quelling excessive hunger and the urge to snack around the clock. Most people probably aren’t eating nearly enough protein, especially as they get on in years.

Primal is a “low-carb diet” in comparison to the Standard American Diet, and similar ilks, in which grains and sugars run rampant. A primary goal of the Primal Blueprint is for you to become fat-adapted, so fat becomes a primary fuel instead of glucose (sugar).

That doesn’t mean Primal is anti-carb. Carbs are fuel, but excessive carb intake leads to chronically high insulin and the health consequences you can expect as a result. Limit your carb intake to nutrient-dense varieties and only in the amount that you need to provide glucose to the brain and fuel your activities. (Hint: it’s less than you probably think.)

The Primal Blueprint Carbohydrate Curve lays out reasonable targets for carb intake.

Learn to love them. Fats are the fuel of choice in the Primal Blueprint. Beside providing energy, they are necessary for certain crucial metabolic functions and have little to no impact on insulin. On a Primal eating style, the plurality of your calories will likely come from fats like avocado oil, olive oil, and the fats naturally occurring in animal products, avocados, coconut products, and nuts and seeds.

Like what you’re reading? This post is public so feel free to share it. Sharing helps more people learn about the Primal Blueprint!

Most popular diets look at overall calories as the main factor in weight loss, weight gain, and, by implication, overall health. They don’t care about where those calories come from. Despite that age-old Conventional Wisdom mantra that "a calorie is a calorie," a calorie is not a calorie. The different macronutrients we eat have different effects in the body, and diets that focus only on calorie restriction miss the point of eating to be healthy, not just skinny.

That doesn’t mean that calories don’t matter. They do. Or rather, it’s important not to intake a lot more energy than you expend over the course of weeks, months, and years. Energy excess is a catalyst for many downstream health issues. I just don’t think calorie counting is the answer for most people.

That’s why the Primal Blueprint doesn’t prescribe specific calorie intakes. Our genes want us to be lean and fit. And that starts with eating from the long list of Primal Blueprint healthy foods and trying to avoid that other list of grain-laden, sugary, processed, and otherwise unhealthy foods.

Remember that our genes are accustomed to the way our ancestors ate: intermittently, sporadically, sometimes in large quantities, and sometimes not at all for days. This random or “non-linear” eating pattern kept their bodies in a constant state of preparedness.

That said, for newcomers to the Primal Blueprint, what you eat is more important than when. There’s no point worrying about carb cycling or optimal meal timing if you’re still eating mostly grains and sugars. That’s all stuff you can worry about down the road.

When you’re ready, I’ve written about intermittent fasting extensively on the blog. As I’ve said many times, when it comes to health and longevity, most of the magic happens when we aren’t eating. It’s important to give your body time to engage in the maintenance, repair, and building processes that are inherent to health and healthy aging. Intermittent fasting has incredible benefits, and you have options when it comes to implementation.

Lastly, seasonal eating isn’t mandatory, but it can be a great way to vary your carb sources and keep your food interesting. If you’re shopping at your local farmer’s markets, you’ll naturally find different options in the summer, fall, winter, and spring.

Although it’s not explicitly covered by the Primal laws, food quality is an important consideration for Primal eating.

In a perfect world, a Primal diet would comprise grass-fed/pastured beef and dairy products, pastured chicken and pork, wild seafood, and produce raised organically or pesticide-free.  The world isn’t perfect, of course, and the “ideal” foods aren’t always available or budget-friendly (although it’s worth pointing out that few things are a better investment of time and money than high-quality food).

The goal of a Primal diet is to eat the best foods you can, understanding that you might need to compromise on organic or buy conventionally raised meat sometimes. Don’t sell yourself short, but also don’t let perfect be the enemy of good.

I started Primal Kitchen in order to make eating real food easier and more delicious. Selfishly, I was sick of having to make all my own condiments at home. I also heard over and over from people in the Primal community that convenience was a major barrier to fully embracing a Primal way of eating. So, I made sauces, dressings, and other pantry staples with ingredients I feel good about so that if you do have to compromise, it won’t be on flavor or ingredient integrity.

The Primal Blueprint, and the book of the same name, offers a framework for achieving your personal best health, vitality, and longevity. To learn more about the other pillars of health, check out the Primal Blueprint movement and lifestyle laws.

Mark’s Daily Apple is still under construction. My team and I are actively working to repost the archives. Check back regularly to find posts that are new to you and revisit old favorites. Looking for a specific post? Let us know here.

* This blog reflects my personal views and opinions and isn’t intended as medical advice, but I hope it will be informative and inspiring as you pursue a healthy, fulfilling life.

#2957. A Surge of Marine Heat Waves Threatens to Scramble Ocean Life

Waters off the coast of Southern California, where a prolonged marine heat wave is disrupting sea life. Kevin Carter / Getty Images

Fueled by climate change and a strengthening El Niño, record-setting marine heat waves are gripping oceans worldwide and playing havoc with sea life. Mounting evidence from recent events suggests the damage could be severe and long-lasting, both onshore and off.

The common octopus is a warm water species that usually lives in the temperate waters of the Mediterranean. But last year, octopus larvae hitchhiked across the English Channel attached to plankton, resulting in an octopus bloom off England’s coast. This year, the bloom is the largest it’s been in 75 years.

Normally, octopus larvae that drift into cold waters die. But this year’s bloom, experts say, is the result of an unusually intense marine heat wave off of Devon and Cornwall, which raised water temperatures up to 9 degrees F above normal.

The change in species has been a boon for local fishers selling octopus into the lucrative European export market. (Octopus landings along the U.K.’s southwest coast were roughly 7,700 percent higher than usual in 2025.) And while the invasion has slashed the catch of scallops, lobster, and crab, it has boosted numbers of seals, conger eels, and dolphins, which eat octopus. The warming, in combination with abundant prey, has also drawn bluefin tuna and sharks to British waters for the first time in decades.

“It has completely shaken up the food chain,” said Bryce Stewart, a senior research fellow at the UK’s Marine Biological Association, which has been studying the phenomenon. While this kind of bloom has happened here before, he said, this one is different. During past surges, the return of cold water killed off the octopuses. This time, the octopuses aren’t dying. They’re reproducing.

Globally, marine heat waves — prolonged periods of unusually warm water — are becoming more common, more intense, longer lasting, and more widespread, experts say. This year, ocean heat is unusually severe, setting new temperature records. According to Global Climate Risks, a nonprofit created by scientists to publicize the effects of climate change, an estimated 82 percent of the world’s oceans are currently experiencing marine heat waves. Evidence from recent hot spells suggests the impacts on sea life could be profound and long-lasting.

Ocean fevers can rearrange marine ecosystems, drive fish to move into deeper, colder layers of the sea, trigger coral bleaching, and cause widespread marine die-offs. They can increase the severity and number of algal blooms, which harm fish, shellfish, marine mammals, and birds. And they can cause extreme weather events, like tropical storms and hurricanes, and upend fishing and tourism.

To officially be called a heat wave, temperatures must rise above the 90th percentile of normal seasonal variation for five consecutive days. While marine heat waves do occur naturally, climate scientists say anthropogenic warming has exacerbated them. According to NOAA scientists, oceans have absorbed more than 90 percent of the excess heat produced by human-caused global warming. One recent study found that, since 1940, warming has led to a threefold increase in the number of days per year with extreme heat at the ocean surface.

Marine heat waves in August. Warmer colors indicate greater severity. NOAA

Climate warming and marine heat waves are separate phenomena with different effects. Ocean warming is gradual and allows more time for species to adapt and evolve. Marine heat waves are acute and produce rapid and extreme changes that give organisms little time to adapt. But the long-term warming trend is making ocean heat more severe.

Climate change has raised the temperature of the waters off the U.K. by around 2 degrees F above average in the last six decades, worsening this year’s record heat. Records have fallen elsewhere in Europe as well. “We find it’s the most extreme July marine heat wave conditions for the Western Mediterranean, the Celtic Region, and for the Bay of Biscay and Iberian Peninsula,” said Catherine Gregory, a climate scientist at the University of Bern.

The Pacific has warmed in northern regions by almost 2 degrees F and is currently experiencing the world’s largest marine heat wave, which sprawls over more than 24 million square miles. And in the central and eastern Pacific, things are bound to get worse. This year’s El Niño is expected to be the strongest in living memory — it has already spiked sea temperatures far earlier in the year than expected — and scientists fear it will expand the size and intensity of the two existing marine heat waves off the California coast. Based on studies of past heat waves — in particular, an event known as the Blob, the largest marine heat wave on global record, which dramatically raised temperatures in the eastern Pacific between 2014 and 2016 — scientists are warily watching the warming ocean, anticipating a cascade of severe impacts both onshore and off.

The Blob began to form in the fall of 2013, when a persistent high-pressure ridge greatly reduced the cool winter winds that normally blow over the eastern Pacific. Without that cooling, the sun created a very large body of very warm water. In 2014, ocean temperatures were 7 degrees F above average, and the cold nutrient-rich water that normally coursed along the West Coast disappeared.

The ecological changes were legion. In 2015, researcher Ric Brodeur was monitoring populations of shrimp-like krill, which form the base of the marine food chain, expecting his sampling nets to be full. Instead, they were almost empty. “You could fit all the krill from the trip into a little plastic bag,” he said.

The lack of food caused humpback whale numbers in the North Pacific to drop by an estimated 7,000 — or 20 percent of the population. Many females became emaciated, which reduced calf survival and caused a reproductive decline that didn’t begin to reverse until 2021, five years after the Blob had officially ended.

While many anomalies caused by the unusually warm water were temporary, some were not. The Blob — which at times approached 10 degrees F higher than average — caused large-scale and long-term ecosystem changes, some of which persist to this day. Researchers are concerned that today’s warming waters will compound the damage caused by previous marine heat waves.

A diver surveys bleached corals off the coast of Koh Tao in Thailand, which was hit by a marine heat wave in 2024. Scientists are worried that record ocean heat this year will further harm corals. Lillian Suwanrumpha / AFP via Getty Images

One of the most egregious of those earlier changes was the die-off of kelp forests along thousands of miles of coast, from Baja California to as far north as British Columbia. Kelp complexes are some of the most dynamic and biologically productive ecosystems on Earth, the “underwater rainforests” of temperate and Arctic coastal oceans. They offer food, nursery grounds, and shelter from predators and rough currents to hundreds of marine species. Kelp canopies, which consist of rubbery floating fronds, are home to sea otters, seabirds, and juvenile fish, while abalone, sea urchins, lobsters, and octopus live on or near the seafloor.

Efforts to save kelp forests from ocean warming are ramping up. Read more.

“The Pacific kelp forest has lasted for millions of years,” said Melissa Carter, a biological oceanographer with UC San Diego’s Scripps Institution of Oceanography, thanks to upwellings of cold water along the coast. “But it has a specific temperature threshold where it falls apart and it can’t survive. And everything that lives on the coast depends on that ecosystem.”

One study found that during the Blob, overall kelp cover dropped considerably: Northern California was hardest hit, with 95 percent of its kelp wiped out. The loss of kelp reshaped the entire food web, decimating red abalone populations, which were already in trouble, and prompting the state of California to extend its ban on recreational fishing of the species at least until April 2036.

The Blob also severely impacted seabirds. Half of Alaska’s common murres — some 4 million birds — starved to death as warmer currents disrupted migrations of anchovies, sardines, and other forage fish. “Recent population abundance estimates since then have found no evidence of recovery,” the authors of a 2024 study of the murres concluded, “suggesting that the heatwave may have led to an ecosystem shift.”

Current marine heat waves are already taking a toll. Dead and dying birds, including California brown pelicans, loons, and grebes, are turning up again on Southern California beaches. “We’re seeing cormorants walk to shore and then just die within the hour,” Tammy Russell, a researcher at the Scripps Institution of Oceanography, told the Associated Press, “and we’re seeing it happening across the whole coast.” Seabirds along South America’s Pacific coast have been dying in large numbers in recent months, and experts have linked the fatalities to higher ocean temperatures.

Researchers are concerned that current record-setting ocean temperatures, intensified by the strengthening El Niño, will further harm corals. Bleaching occurs when rising water temperatures, in addition to other stressors like pollution and overexposure to sunlight, spur corals to release their symbiotic algae, which harvest energy for them. A bleaching event that lasted from early 2023 until mid-2025 affected more than 84 percent of the world’s coral reef areas. In a major report that was just released, Global Coral Reef Monitoring Network researchers warn that coral reefs around the world are seeing a rapid increase in bleaching and that recovery windows between disturbance events are narrowing.

Veterinarian Jennifer Russell treats an ailing pelican at SeaWorld in San Diego. Rehabilitation centers have cared for hundreds of emaciated seabirds amid a marine heat wave in Southern California. Gregory Bull / AP Photo

Marine heat waves can affect conditions throughout the water column. When a cap of warm water forms at the ocean’s surface, it prevents vertical mixing and can promote the growth of harmful algal species. The explosion of aquatic organisms depletes oxygen in the water, blocks sunlight, and can release harmful toxins into the air and water.

One of the largest algal blooms in history now stretches from Southern California to the coast of British Columbia. The bloom is dominated by an alga called Pseudo-nitzschia, which multiplies faster and becomes more toxic in warmer water. The alga produces a neurotoxin called domoic acid that is responsible for killing hundreds of California’s sea lions, whales, and porpoises.

Supertrawlers are taking Antarctic krill that whales depend on. Read more.

It is a perilous moment for the world’s oceans as temperatures hit levels never seen in modern times. And scientists fear the impacts of earlier warming events are only a prologue for what lies ahead.

“The Blob was extreme, the warmest we’ve ever seen anything in 110 years,” said Carter, of Scripps. “We are definitely along the same trend now, and the number of marine heat wave days we’ve [already] seen are exactly on par with 2015. This is a new Blob.”

Jim Robbins is a veteran journalist based in Helena, Montana. A regular contributor to Yale Environment 360, he has written for the New York Times, Conde Nast Traveler, and numerous other publications. His latest book is the The Wonder of Birds: What They Tell Us About the World, Ourselves and a Better Future. More about Jim Robbins →

#2958. Easy Science Experiments for World Book Day

As World Book Day is just around the corner, I thought I’d pull together some of my favourite book themed science experiments. Here at Science Sparks, we love using a book to help bring a science activity to life, and if I’m ever short of inspiration, reading a great children’s book always helps me come up with something new.

I’d love you to take a scroll through my ideas, and whether you’re making potions for George’s Marvellous Medicine, building a bridge for the Billy Goats Gruff or building a candy house for a witch, my hands-on, inexpensive science activity ideas are sure to make for a memorable World Book Day.

My Gruffalo’s child shadow puppets are super easy to make and great fun to make shadows with. Try holding the shadow puppets and torch in different positions to investigate how the shape of the shadow changes.

Shadow puppets also work for Peter Pan, who loses his shadow, which Wendy then sews back on.

Make a model of a butterfly life cycle with my free template for The Very Hungry Caterpillar. Pasta or sweets/candy work well for this activity.

Discover how different materials have different properties and why, while you help Ariel sort her cave of treasures.

Discover the best materials for making a Stickman, or try to find the perfect Stickman shaped stick!

Our Lorax or Cat in the Hat themed sensory trays are bright and colourful enough to keep little ones busy for a while. Use magnets and tweezers for a bit of variation.

Stop Incy Wincy Spider falling down the water spout by finding a way to stick her to the top or build Incy Wincy an umbrella.

Make a washing line of pants for Aliens LOVE Underpants, and test how waterproof they are! Or, try catapulting pants!

My Roald Dahl themed fine motor skill activities are great fun and can be used over and over again. My favourite is Mr Twit’s wormy spaghetti!

First up for 5-10 year olds has to be our Roald Dahl science experiments. For Charlie and the Chocolate Factory, the good old skittles investigation is always impressive, or make gummy sweets bigger by leaving them in water for a couple of hours. We always enjoy making a house from sweets, too.

Make fun, fizzy potions like George and his Marvellous Medicine.

Learn about camouflage ( The Enormous Crocodile ) with this gorgeous camoflage art activity from Fireflies and Mudpies.

Make a homemade worm hotel for James and the Giant Peach, or investigate to find out how many helium balloons it takes to lift a peach.

Stack books on an eggshell bridge for Matilda.

Try mirror writing for Alice in Wonderland or reverse the direction of arrows using a glass of water!

These Fairy Tale themed experiment printables include making a raft for the Billy Goats Gruff, growing a bean for Jack and more!

Build and test houses for The Three Little Pigs to find which is the strongest.

Create a Whisper-Ma-Phone for the Lorax to learn about sound waves. Investigate to see if the phone works around corners or if a longer string makes it better or worse.

Leave messages for Hansel and Gretel using invisible ink.

Build a bed for the Princess and the Pea. This is a great little engineering STEM challenge, beautifully themed around the story.

Build Cinderella a new carriage! We made a balloon-powered car with a balloon pumpkin.

Make a zip line for Jack, grow him a new beanstalk, clean his coins or make a musical harp. My Jack and the Beanstalk pack with 5 activities is FREE to download.

Finally, try one of my Fairy Tale Science activity ideas. You can make a zip wire for Jack, a pulley for Rapunzel, a candy house for Hansel and Gretel, a gingerbread house for The Gingerbread Man and more.

If you’re looking for an easy World Book Day craft, these fun corner bookmarks from Red Ted Art could be perfect!

#2959. From Gangs to Grizzlies

How a former gang member found a different kind of vigilance in the Montana wild.

One summer evening, I was hiking a trail near Marias Pass in Glacier National Park, returning to the car after a few hours of wildlife photography at a lake— nothing but deer and mosquitoes to show for it—when I noticed something blondish-brown moving toward me. It barely registered. I felt relaxed, though, truthfully, I’m never fully relaxed.

A grizzly cub, big as an overgrown Saint Bernard. I was fascinated—until reality hit. The mother wouldn’t be far behind.

“Hey, bear,” I shouted, my voice cracking. The cub woofed an alarm and took off to my left, a dark blur swallowed by the forest.

I turned toward the trail ahead. And—boom—there she was. Running at me, big and fast and flat to the ground.

I grabbed the bear spray strapped to my chest, fumbled the can free, and held it out like a pistol. She closed in. I could see the whites

#2960. 17 Stylish, Woodsy-Chic Catskills Airbnbs for Every Season

As a lifelong New Yorker, the Catskills have been my go-to escape from New York City for as long as I can remember, and I’ve yet to run out of reasons to return to the Airbnbs in the area. There are ski towns that come alive with the first snowfall, Main Streets lined with antique shops and old-school diners, and miles of mountain roads that (somehow) turn even more lovely when the leaves change color.

Just a couple of hours north of the city, this four-county region of southeastern New York has been luring urbanites into the mountains for centuries. In the 19th century, grand resorts drew vacationers seeking fresh air and scenery; later, the Catskills were known for their summer camps and folk music, with the southern region earning the nickname the Borscht Belt. Today, that storied past mingles with a new generation of creative small towns, farm-to-table restaurants, and design-minded hideaways that make a weekend away feel like a proper change of pace.

Finding the right Airbnb in the Catskills is almost as fun as the actual visit: Across the region, you'll find glass-walled A-frames tucked into the forest, renovated farmhouses with room for the whole family, and modern cabins where the wood-burning stoves practically beg you to cancel your dinner plans. Some are minutes from hiking trails, swimming holes, and ski slopes; others put you close to beloved towns like Woodstock, Phoenicia, and Tannersville. Whatever the season, these Catskills vacation rentals make a wonderful starting point for your next upstate adventure.

Read on for 17 of the most beloved, top-rated, and still-bookable Airbnbs in the Catskills, per a city gal who's already added them all to her wishlist.

What's the best town or region to stay in the Catskills?

There's no “best” town in the Catskills, but there's certainly some to keep on your radar, depending on your preferences. To the north, towns like Hudson, Catskill, and Tivoli skew artsy and architectural, where design galleries sit beside unfussy yet thoughtful restaurants. To the west, Woodstock and Phoenicia thrum with trailheads, river bends, vintage shops, and old-school diners. Further into the mountains, Shandaken and Big Indian are known for their sense of seclusion and access to nature—favorites of hikers, writers, and anyone looking to go quiet for a while. Skiers head to Windham and Hunter for well-groomed slopes and slow evenings by the fire.

#2962. How to Become a Digital Nomad from the Philippines: A 12-Month Plan With Real Peso Numbers

Your first foreign client pays USD 900 for the month. At today’s rate that is about PHP 56,000, more than a lot of office jobs in Manila, and suddenly the laptop-on-a-beach thing feels close. Then you look up Malaysia’s nomad visa and see USD 24,000 a year. Thailand’s wants half a million baht sitting in the bank. The dream and the paperwork are on two different planets.

They don’t have to be. You just need to do things in the right order.

This guide is for Filipinos who want to work online and travel: fresh grads, BPO agents eyeing freelancing, VAs who already have clients, and OFWs thinking about coming home without giving up a foreign income. It shows how to become a digital nomad from the Philippines in four stages over 12 months: the income you need at each stage in pesos, the papers NAIA immigration looks for, and when a real nomad visa becomes realistic. Every rule and fee is dated and sourced.

A few links in this guide are affiliate links. If you book through them we may earn a small commission at no extra cost to you, and we only link to things we would use ourselves.

This is general information, not tax, legal or immigration advice. Rules change, so confirm with the BIR, the Bureau of Immigration or the embassy concerned, or a licensed professional, before you act on anything here.

Here is the whole plan in one place, as of October 2026, at USD 1 = PHP 62.60 (ECB reference rate, 2 October 2026).

The order matters more than the speed. Skip the boring middle step and you get stuck at the immigration counter or in a visa refusal.

This plan assumes you hold a Philippine passport, you live in the Philippines now, and you will earn from clients or an employer outside the Philippines. That last part is what makes you a digital nomad rather than a tourist with a laptop.

It fits VAs, writers, designers, developers, marketers and support agents who can work fully online, BPO agents ready to go direct to clients, and OFWs coming home who want to keep a foreign-currency income.

It is not the right plan if you need a stable salary from month one, if your work requires you on site, or if you are hoping to work for a company in the country you visit. Every visa-free entry and every nomad visa in this guide bans local employment. You can work for your foreign clients. You cannot take a job at a Bangkok cafe.

One more thing to get straight early. A digital nomad is not an OFW in the eyes of the government. You will not have an OEC, you will not be covered by OWWA as a land-based worker, and the tax breaks OFWs get do not apply to you. We explain why in our guide to travel insurance for Filipino digital nomads, and it changes how you plan your safety net.

The first stage is not about travel at all. It is about getting paid in dollars, reliably, from home.

Pick one skill you can sell this month, not five you might learn next year. The roles that land fastest for Filipinos are general virtual assistance, customer support, social media management, bookkeeping, video editing and writing. If you already have BPO experience, lead with it. Foreign clients know what “three years handling US customer calls” means, and it is worth more to them than a certificate.

Where Filipinos actually find first clients:

What should you expect to earn? HireTalent.ph’s 2026 salary guide puts full-time Filipino remote workers here, converted at PHP 62.60:

Those are full-time figures at 8 hours a day, 22 days a month. Part-time first clients pay less, so the realistic target for month three is one steady client covering your basic monthly costs at home.

Set up how you get paid before you need it. Most Filipino freelancers receive through PayPal, Payoneer or Wise, then cash out to GCash, Maya or a Philippine bank. Fees and exchange rates differ a lot between them, so run one real invoice through each before you commit.

From day one, save every contract, invoice and payment receipt in one folder. Later, that folder is your proof of income for immigration, the BIR and a visa application.

This is the step most “become a digital nomad” guides skip, and that includes our own five-step version from 2025. It is also the one that decides whether you get through immigration smoothly and whether a visa officer believes your income later.

Register with the BIR as self-employed. Resident citizens are taxed on income from anywhere, including your foreign clients. You register through the BIR’s Online Registration and Update System (ORUS) using Form 1901 and get a Certificate of Registration, Form 2303. Since 22 January 2024, the old PHP 500 annual registration fee is no longer collected, under the Ease of Paying Taxes Act (RA 11976). We have a full walkthrough of how Filipino digital nomads register with the BIR and pay taxes.

Decide on the 8% option. Under Revenue Regulations No. 8-2018, self-employed individuals with gross receipts of PHP 3 million or less a year can choose to pay 8% on gross receipts above PHP 250,000, instead of the graduated rates. For most freelancers on one or two clients it is simpler, and often cheaper, but run your own numbers. You choose it when you register or on your first quarterly return of the year.

Start PhilHealth as a self-earning member. Since January 2025, the premium is 5% of monthly income, with a PHP 500 minimum. PhilHealth’s own examples: PHP 30,000 a month means PHP 1,500, and PHP 100,000 means PHP 5,000. SSS and Pag-IBIG also accept self-employed and voluntary members, and both are worth keeping up for retirement and housing loans, but they will not pay your hospital bill in Chiang Mai.

Why bother before you travel? Because now you can show four documents proving a real, taxed income: your BIR Certificate of Registration, your client contract, three months of bank statements, and your latest income tax return once filed. That bundle is your passport within the passport.

Now you travel, but small. A visa-free stay in Southeast Asia lets you test working across a time zone, on hotel wifi, in a new city, without paying for a visa.

Current visa-free stays for Philippine passports in the region that work well for a first trip:

Be clear-eyed about what these entries are. They are tourist entries. None of these countries has a visa-free category for remote work, which is exactly why nomad visas exist. Keep your trip short, don’t take local work, and don’t present yourself to an immigration officer as “working in Thailand”. You are a tourist who happens to keep serving clients back home.

Getting past NAIA immigration. The 2023 Revised IACAT Guidelines on Departure Formalities, in force since 3 September 2023, require every traveler to show a passport, a visa where needed, a boarding pass and, where necessary, a return ticket. Self-funded tourists can be asked for proof of accommodation, proof of financial capacity or source of income, and proof of employment. For a freelancer, “proof of employment” is the hardest part, and that is where your BIR Certificate of Registration, client contract and bank statements earn their keep. Our guide to avoiding offloading as an online freelancer covers the questions officers ask.

Picking your first base. Choose somewhere with a short flight, a big community of remote workers and easy wifi. Da Nang is a strong first pick: Cebu Pacific, Philippine Airlines and Philippines AirAsia fly it nonstop from Manila in under three hours, and you get beach and city in one. The An Thuong area near My Khe Beach is where a lot of remote workers end up, with cafes that expect laptops. You can compare Manila to Da Nang fares on Trip.com, and browse apartments and hotels near An Thuong for a two or three week stay.

Sort your connection before you land. A regional eSIM means your phone works the minute the plane touches down, which matters when a client message comes in during immigration. Check Airalo’s Asia eSIM plans, which cover Vietnam, Thailand and Malaysia on one plan.

And remember travel tax. As of October 2026 the Philippine travel tax is still PHP 1,620 for economy. The House passed a bill to abolish it in March 2026, but it is not law until the Senate passes it and it takes effect, so check whether your airline already collected it.

Here is what nobody tells beginners: most nomad visas are built for incomes well above what a first-year Filipino freelancer earns. That is not a reason to quit. It is a reason to know your target number.

The two nomad visas that make the most sense for a Filipino, and what they demand as of October 2026:

Malaysia DE Rantau Nomad Pass. Open to all nationalities except Israel, so Filipinos qualify, per MDEC’s official FAQ. The income test is USD 24,000 a year for tech talent (about PHP 1.5 million, or PHP 125,000 a month) and USD 60,000 for non-tech roles. MDEC’s tech list includes digital marketers and digital content creators, not only developers. A general VA role is not on it, so check where your job title falls before you apply. Freelancers are explicitly allowed, with client contracts, three months of invoices, proof of payment and three months of bank statements. The pass runs 3 to 12 months and can be renewed to a total of 24 months. You also need health insurance covering Malaysia for the whole pass. MDEC is currently warning applicants of processing delays, so apply well ahead.

For the insurance, SafetyWing’s nomad plan covers Malaysia and runs as a subscription you can stop when you head home.

See what SafetyWing costs for a stay in Malaysia

Thailand Destination Thailand Visa (DTV). No monthly income figure. Instead you need THB 500,000 in savings (about PHP 932,000), plus proof of remote work for foreign clients. The visa is valid five years, with 180 days per entry. Applications go through the official Thai e-Visa portal. The fee on the Manila checklist we last checked was PHP 18,000.

So where do you stand? Using the salary bands above:

For the full list of programmes that do and don’t accept a Philippine passport, and the traps that quietly exclude us, see our guide to digital nomad visas Filipinos can actually get.

Rates as of 2 October 2026: USD 1 = PHP 62.60, MYR 1 = PHP 15.33, THB 1 = PHP 1.86.

The DE Rantau processing fee is non-refundable even if you are refused, per MDEC, which is one more reason not to apply until your documents are solid.

Leaving before the paperwork exists. Flying out with no BIR registration, no contract and no bank trail is how freelancers get pulled aside at NAIA. The trip is not the hard part. Proving your income is.

Treating a tourist entry like a work permit. Saying “I work here online” at a foreign immigration counter can go badly. You are a visitor.

Assuming you are now an OFW. You are not, unless you are deployed with an OEC and a foreign employer. Without OFW status, you stay liable to the BIR and you don’t get OWWA’s coverage.

Applying for a visa at the minimum. If your income is exactly at the threshold, one slow month in your bank statements can sink the application. Aim for a cushion of at least 10 to 20% above the line.

No backup internet. Brownouts and typhoons don’t care about deadlines. Keep a pocket wifi or second SIM, and a coworking space you can run to.

Can Filipinos become digital nomads? Yes. The main limits are visa rules and proof of income, not the passport itself. Filipinos qualify for several nomad visas, including Malaysia’s DE Rantau and Thailand’s DTV.

How much do I need to earn to be a digital nomad from the Philippines? To travel visa-free in Southeast Asia, enough to cover your costs abroad plus your bills at home, with a few months of savings as a buffer. There is no official minimum for that stage. For Malaysia’s DE Rantau tech pass, USD 24,000 a year, about PHP 1.5 million, as of October 2026.

Do I need to register with the BIR as a freelancer? Resident citizens are taxed on worldwide income, so yes, self-employed freelancers are expected to register and file. The 8% option is available if your gross receipts are PHP 3 million or less.

Can I work remotely in Thailand on a visa-free entry? The 30-day exemption is for tourism. If you want to work remotely there for months, the DTV is the visa designed for it.

What documents should a freelancer bring to NAIA? Passport, return ticket and booking details, plus proof of income: BIR Certificate of Registration, client contract, recent bank statements and your latest ITR.

Becoming a digital nomad from the Philippines is less about the plane ticket and more about what you build at home first: one solid skill, a foreign client who pays on time, your BIR papers, and a bank trail that proves it. Do that and the first trip is easy. Keep growing your income and the nomad visa stops being a fantasy and becomes a form you fill in.

So this week, do one thing: pick your skill and send five applications. Month three comes faster than you think, kabayan.

Save this guide for later: pin one of these to your Pinterest board.

#2963. Scientists finally uncover why statins can cause muscle pain

Statins are among the most important medications for preventing cardiovascular disease. By lowering cholesterol, they have helped save millions of lives and reduce the likelihood of heart attacks and strokes. However, these benefits can come with unwanted effects. Some patients develop muscle pain or weakness, while a small number experience serious muscle breakdown that can potentially cause kidney failure.

Scientists at the University of British Columbia (UBC), working with colleagues at the University of Wisconsin-Madison, have identified a molecular mechanism that helps explain these complications. Their findings, published in Nature Communications, could guide the development of safer statins that maintain their cholesterol-lowering benefits while minimizing muscle damage.

How Statins Trigger a Harmful Calcium Leak

To investigate what happens inside muscle cells, the researchers used cryo-electron microscopy, an advanced imaging method that allows scientists to examine protein structures in remarkable detail. Their investigation centered on the ryanodine receptor (RyR1), a protein that regulates the movement of calcium within muscle cells.

Under normal conditions, this protein functions as a controlled gateway, releasing calcium when muscles need to contract. The researchers found that statins can interfere with this process by holding the channel open. As a result, calcium escapes continuously into areas where it should be carefully regulated, creating conditions that can injure muscle tissue.

"We were able to see, almost atom by atom, how statins latch onto this channel," said lead author Dr. Steven Molinarolo, a postdoctoral researcher in UBC's department of biochemistry and molecular biology. "That leak of calcium explains why some patients experience muscle pain or, in extreme cases, life-threatening complications."

Three Statin Molecules Reveal the Mechanism Behind Muscle Damage

The researchers concentrated on atorvastatin, a commonly prescribed medication for lowering cholesterol. Although this particular drug was the main focus, their results suggest that similar interactions could occur with other medications in the statin family.

One of the most striking discoveries was the unusual way statin molecules attach to the calcium channel. Rather than acting individually, three molecules occupy a specific pocket within the protein. The first binds while the channel is closed, making it more likely to open. Two additional molecules then enter the pocket and hold the channel open, allowing calcium to continue leaking.

"This is the first time we've had a clear picture of how statins activate this channel," said Dr. Filip Van Petegem, senior author and professor at UBC's Life Sciences Institute. "It's a big step forward because it gives us a roadmap for designing statins that don't interact with muscle tissue."

This detailed understanding could give drug developers a way to address the problem at its source. By modifying the specific features of statin molecules that interfere with muscle proteins, scientists may be able to reduce unwanted effects without compromising the drugs' ability to control cholesterol.

A Potential Path Toward Safer Cholesterol Drugs

More than 200 million people worldwide use statins. Although severe muscle injury occurs in only a small percentage of patients, less serious problems such as soreness, weakness, and fatigue are considerably more widespread. These symptoms can be disruptive enough that some people discontinue their medication, potentially losing important protection against cardiovascular disease.

The discovery could eventually help researchers create treatments that are easier to tolerate, allowing more patients to continue taking medications that reduce their risk of heart attacks and strokes.

Achieving this level of understanding required exceptionally detailed imaging. Working with the high-resolution macromolecular cryo-electron microscopy facility at UBC's faculty of medicine, the team was able to observe precisely how statin molecules interact with the muscle protein. These structural insights connect a longstanding drug safety concern to a specific molecular process, providing valuable information for future medication development.

"Statins have been a cornerstone of cardiovascular care for decades," Dr. Van Petegem said. "Our goal is to make them even safer, so patients can benefit without fear of serious side effects."

Although additional research and drug development will be needed before these findings translate into new treatments, they offer a promising direction. For millions of people who depend on statins to protect their hearts, future medications with fewer muscle-related complications could make treatment more comfortable and improve everyday quality of life.

Materials provided by University of British Columbia. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.
