#2965. Data Centers are Helping Fuel a U.S. Battery Boom

Batteries power a data center near Reno, Nevada. Courtesy of Redwood Materials

Across the U.S., tech companies have courted backlash for using polluting gas generators to power massive new data centers. But energy industry data shows that in addition to building out natural gas, firms are adding huge amounts of battery storage.

The energy demands of data centers are so great that large projects may need approval from regulators before connecting to the power grid. But instead of waiting for that approval, tech companies keen to build the infrastructure for A.I. are often building gas generators on site. By one estimate, more than 100 gigawatts of onsite gas power, the rough equivalent of 100 large gas power plants, is planned for data centers across the U.S.

To take full advantage of those gas generators, some companies are installing large lithium-ion batteries alongside, according to a new report from the Solar Energy Industries Association and Benchmark Mineral Intelligence. Batteries are also being employed in tandem with renewable energy.

The report finds that data centers accounted for 5 of the nearly 31 gigawatt-hours of battery capacity installed nationally in the first half of this year. Further, data centers accounted for 75 percent of the capacity installed at commercial or industrial sites. Looking ahead, analysts project that data center demand for batteries will grow fivefold by the end of this decade.

In some places, the A.I. boom is also spurring installations of home batteries, as data centers obtain permission to connect to the grid by funding the installation of batteries in nearby homes. Under such programs, homeowners agree to discharge their batteries to the grid as needed to meet spikes in demand. Analysts say that public outcry over the data center boom may push companies to fund more home battery installations.

In a similar vein, some companies are looking to meet the energy needs of data centers by providing rooftop solar to surrounding households or by paying to weatherize homes to curb power demand from heating and cooling. New polling from the Yale Program on Climate Change Communication finds that 73 percent of Americans support requiring new data centers to offset their electricity use by installing rooftop solar or weatherizing nearby homes.

In a recent interview with Yale Environment 360, former Energy Department official Jigar Shah said that it may ultimately be cheaper for tech companies to weatherize homes than to install gas turbines on site, to say nothing of the impact on public opinion. “At the end of the day, these [companies] need public permission,” he said. Shah added, “Figuring out how to gain that permission I think is also something the data centers have to solve.”

How Batteries, Not Natural Gas, Can Power the Data Center Boom

→ Baca artikel asli

#2966. Primal Grandparenting: Tips and Thoughts

I’m a grandpa now. Twice over. My daughter Devyn has a girl and boy. I have a granddaughter and grandson. Over the last couple decades, it’s represented the single most impactful change in how I view myself in the grand scheme of things—far more even than the sale of Primal Kitchen. I still remember the day I met my granddaughter. Looking down at that little girl, barely aware of anything going on, I realized that something enormous had happened. Things would never be the same again.

We talk a ton about gene expression around here. The entire Primal Blueprint is designed around leveraging the environment to create the best possible stimulus for your genes. Genes turn on and off based on the environmental stimuli they encounter—the food you eat, the sleep you get, the sun you expose yourself to, the exercise you do, the chemicals you interact with. What might look like a “bad gene” on paper can be mitigated, nullified, or even turned into a positive with the right environmental stimuli. And more often than not, emulating an ancestral environment will trigger those positive changes to gene expression and set you up for good health, fitness, and happiness.

If something as simple as the oil you use to cook can alter gene expression, what about meeting your grandkid for the first time?

Because having kids yourself is one thing. It’s very cool, extremely transcendent, changes your life, and you'll never be the same, but there's a real generational transition of the mind that takes place when your kids have kids.

Your genes finally have a sense of closure. "You've made it. You've really made it." Because after all, from a natural selection perspective, having kids yourself isn't enough. Passing on your genes to the next generation isn't sufficient. No, your genes want to see themselves carried over to the next-next generation too, just so you “know” that the line will continue. It hasn’t just made it one more generation—it’s made it two more. That presages good things down the line. That's the promised land for your heritage. You mean to tell me your genes don't sense the shift?

The first thought is how quickly life passes. It feels like Devyn was 12 only a few years ago and we were attending soccer games, working on class projects and boogie boarding with her brother Kyle at Zuma Beach in Malibu. Now she's a wife and a mother and on her way to building her own family. The time compression that occurs retroactively in the mind seems almost unfair. Did I give enough? Did I appreciate or enjoy it enough in real time?  And then, of course, here I am now at 70 playing Ultimate Frisbee and standup paddling and fat tire biking and getting after it in the gym. So at another level, my Primal mind fights the reality that I'm a grandfather and that maybe it's time to back off a bit. Because becoming a grandfather is a big signal that you've done your part, fulfilled your role. And then what comes next?

The second thought is how miraculous life is; how this six pound bundle of joy and love came to be a person so quickly and perfectly. I didn't give it as much thought—or perspective—when my own kids were born. Now all of a sudden it boggles my mind and prompts deeper reflection on the real meanings of life, love, responsibility, and purpose. And this from a guy who is all about biology, evolution and epigenetics, and who has always had an answer based purely in science for just about everything.

Holding that little girl made me believe miracles do happen. I still don't know the provenance of those miracles, I don't know the source or whether they can be explained in rational terms, but they absolutely exist.

Becoming a grandparent also thrusts new roles upon you. You're a parent again, but a different sort of parent. You have a new job to do, and you'd better make the most of it and do the best job you can.

You don’t want to be the grandparent that’s relegated to a walker or a hospital bed or a wheelchair, or even just the couch. You want to be the vigorous grandparent who can play with their grandkids. Throwing the football around. Getting down to play tea party. Throwing them in the air, taking them on adventures, going on hikes.

You need to stay fit, stay active, and stay strong. Be neither feeble nor frail.

The classic role of the grandparent is to pass down knowledge. That could be physical know-how—teaching skills like fixing cars, carpentry, or cooking. That could be wisdom—imparting important life lessons and transmitting advice from an age now lost to time.

This is more than tradition and culture. This is the biological role of the grandparent. It’s why grandparents were preserved in the human species. It’s why we don’t just drop dead once we cease being reproductively viable. We still have a role to play, and imparting knowledge and wisdom to our grandkids is it.

Fill that role. You made it this far, which means you have something to pass down to your grandkids. Figure out what that might be, and make it a point to pass it down.

One important role of the grandparent is to spoil the grandkids, but you have to do it properly.

Spoil without spoiling. Expose them to the finer, more pleasurable things in life without making them sick of it or overdoing it. You want to make them happy, provide stuff that mom or dad won't, without over-saturating them. And when you spoil, use only the best quality.

Give high quality ice cream instead of McDonald’s fake ice cream.

Give good chocolate instead of chewy candy that sticks to your teeth.

Watch a classic kid’s movie with them on the couch instead of plunking them down in front of Netflix.

“Grandma’s house” is more than a physical space. It’s an emotional one, a barrier from all that is difficult in the world. If a child’s home is both a place to shelter from the world and be challenged to dominate it, where the child trains to rise in the world, Grandma’s house is purely a shelter. Peace and warmth distilled, refined, concentrated. Grandma’s house is just comfortable. comfortable, warm, inviting, and safe.

When you can, take the grandkids for an evening or overnight. Without enough free time to cultivate their marriage, the marriage suffers and the family (your grandkids) suffers. Help out with childcare whenever possible so that your son or daughter have the space needed to keep their marriage strong.

Are you a grandparent? I'd be curious to hear your tips on Primal grandparenting and insights on what the role has meant in your life.

Mark’s Daily Apple is still under construction. My team and I are actively working to repost the archives. Check back regularly to find posts that are new to you and revisit old favorites. Looking for a specific post? Let us know here.

* This blog reflects my personal views and opinions and isn’t intended as medical advice, but I hope it will be informative and inspiring as you pursue a healthy, fulfilling life.

#2967. Easy Ideas for British Science Week 2026

British Science Week is an annual event celebrating science, technology, engineering and maths. It’s always a wonderfully inspirational 10 days full of smiling faces and great science.

This year, the theme is Curiosity: What’s your question?

There are some brilliant packs full of activity ideas on the British Science Week website, but I thought I’d share some activities on Science Sparks that also fit the theme.

Find out how penguins survive in extreme cold with a simple science activity using ice and lard!

Use a simple maths trick to find the height of a tree!

You’ll need a sunny day for this one, but a DIY sundial is easy to make with just a few simple materials.

Learn about the hollow bones of a bird and the clever adaptations that help them have the energy to fly!

Find out why plants are green and make a model of a plant cell.

Find out why food goes bad and how to preserve it with a fun science activity using apples.

Find out how humans smell and why it affects taste with a simple science activity using jelly beans!

Find out how lungs work with a hands-on lung model.

How do we know to move our hand when we touch something very hot?

Learn about the nervous system and reflex responses with a model neuron.

Find out what happens if you don’t clean your teeth with a simple activity using vinegar and eggs!

Find out how to find the North Star in the sky with this easy space science activity.

Map out the solar system to a scale you can recreate with this simple space science challenge!

Make a simple model of a solar eclipse in this easy space science activity.

Find out if cows contribute to global warming and try a fun science cow burping demo too!

Use a clever trick to make a circle from a piece of square paper!

Eggs spin differently depending on whether they are boiled or raw. A raw egg wobbles and a boiled egg spins beautifully!

Learn about elasticity with a fun bouncy ball demonstration!

Does toast really always land butter side down? Find out with a fun kitchen science investigation.

#2969. The Best Hotels in Hanoi for Design, Dining, and a Sense of Place

In Hanoi, 2,000 years of history continue to shape a city changing at remarkable speed. French colonial villas stand beside centuries-old temples, narrow Old Quarter alleyways give way to wide boulevards, and street-food stalls and family-run cafés share the streets with big-name retailers and cool-kid art galleries.

For many visitors, Vietnam’s capital is often defined by its ancient past. But the best hotels in Hanoi tell a more complete story. They range from iconic Indochine-era landmarks to bold contemporary expressions of Vietnamese maximalism to intimate boutiques set in the midst of the city's everyday rhythms. Increasingly, they're also destinations in their own right, with Michelin-starred restaurants, award-winning cocktail bars, and design-centric spas that rank among the best in the world.

The result is one of Asia's most rewarding and fascinating hotel scenes. Whether you're checking into a luxury landmark or a boutique property with refreshingly reasonable rates, choosing where to stay isn't simply about location or amenities. It's about deciding which Hanoi you want to experience.

Inspired by the nearby Hanoi Opera House, the Bill Bensley–designed Capella Hanoi imagines each of its 47 rooms and suites as the private residence of an opera legend, layering more than 1,000 pieces of vintage memorabilia, hand-painted portraits, and Art Deco flourishes into one gloriously maximalist fantasy. Yet beneath all the spectacle lies an exceptionally comfortable boutique hotel, where Michelin-starred dining, one of Hanoi's best cocktail bars, and the spectacular La Grotta indoor pool are matched by warm, intuitive service from the hotel's “Capella Culturists.” All in all, it's a bold, whimsical counterpoint to the city's more traditional grand hotels.

More than a century after opening its doors in 1901, Sofitel Legend Metropole Hanoi remains the city's defining luxury hotel. Part French colonial landmark, part living museum, the property has hosted everyone from Charlie Chaplin and Graham Greene to diplomats, royals, and world leaders, yet it never feels frozen in time. The Heritage Wing in the original three-story building remains the star, with hardwood floors, ceiling fans, and a distinctly Indochine atmosphere, while six restaurants and bars—including the storied Le Beaulieu and ever-busy Le Club Bar—keep the hotel firmly woven into Hanoi life. Book the complimentary Path of History Tour to descend into the hotel's preserved wartime bomb shelter, then return for cocktails beneath the rattan-fitted Bamboo Bar.

The brainchild of architect Carlos Zapata, whose striking design draws inspiration from Vietnam's coastline and is meant to look like a dragon rising beside the water, the 450-room JW Marriott Hotel Hanoi has become one of the capital's defining business hotels. Its generous rooms, enormous eighth-floor indoor lap pool, and warm, intuitive service make it a rewarding retreat from the city's frenetic center, while standout dining—from the upscale French Grill to one of Hanoi's most expansive breakfast buffets—ensures there's plenty of reason to stay in. For travelers who value space, comfort, and a quieter setting, it's an easy recommendation.

Fairmont's long-awaited Vietnam debut arrived in 2026 with all the splendor of a new luxury landmark. The hotel's spacious, apartment-like rooms and suites, destination-worthy restaurants (led by celebrity chef Luke Nguyen and Michelin-recognized Nicolas Isnard), and the sprawling Cirua Spa immediately place it among Hanoi's top places to stay. Rather than looking backward to the city's colonial past, Fairmont embraces a contemporary expression of Vietnamese luxury through locally inspired design, exceptional wellness, and polished service.

The recently refreshed Hotel de l'Opéra embraces the theatricality of the nearby Hanoi Opera House, with 107 velvet-clad rooms and suites, opera-inspired interiors, and one of the city’s most striking hotel restaurants, Lautrec, overlooked by a soaring glass atrium. While the neighboring Sofitel Legend Metropole is steeped in colonial history, this boutique property offers a more contemporary interpretation of French elegance, complemented by a standout afternoon tea and a classic absinthe bar. The hotel itself has become something of a backdrop for Hanoi's young people, who frequently gather outside to photograph its distinctly European façade.

As the flagship of one of Vietnam's best-loved boutique hotel groups, La Siesta Premium Lakeside pairs contemporary comfort and deeply personal hospitality with one of Hanoi's best locations. Overlooking Hoàn Kiếm Lake, the 52-room boutique hotel combines thoughtfully designed rooms, an excellent rooftop wellness deck, and one of the city's strongest boutique spas specializing in traditional Vietnamese treatments. Step outside and you're immediately on the banks of Hoàn Kiếm Lake, the symbolic heart of Hanoi. The Old Quarter, French Quarter, St. Joseph's Cathedral, Ngọc Sơn Temple, and many of Hanoi's best cafés, cocktail bars, and street-food vendors are all within easy walking distance. Later in the day, the Link Bistro restaurant opens onto the boulevard, making it a pleasant place for coffee or a glass of wine while watching the rhythm of the city unfold.

Set near the Hanoi Train Street, the locally owned Hotel de Lagom takes a refreshingly understated approach to luxury. Across 68 rooms, Scandinavian-accented interiors are softened with Vietnamese craftsmanship, while excellent soundproofing, a rooftop infinity pool, and attentive service make it feel like a calm retreat just beyond the Old Quarter's busiest streets. It's notably one of the better-value boutique stays in the city, with rooms starting under $200. Upgrade to a balcony room if your budget allows—the outdoor space transforms the experience, offering a peaceful perch above the city that's especially rewarding in the early morning or around sunset.

What is the best area for tourists to stay in Hanoi?

For first-time visitors, I almost always recommend staying around Hoàn Kiếm Lake, the city’s beating heart where the historic French Quarter and Old Quarter meet. You'll be within walking distance of Hanoi's biggest cultural landmarks and museums, plus excellent restaurants, cafés, cocktail bars, and shopping—all while still having easy access to the rest of the city. Luxury travelers should look toward the French Quarter around the Opera House, while those who prefer a quieter boutique experience can stay just beyond the Old Quarter at properties like Hotel de Lagom.

If I had to choose just one, I'd recommend Sofitel Legend Metropole Hanoi for its unmatched history, service, dining, and sense of place. More than 120 years after opening, it remains the city's defining grande dame, with beautifully restored Heritage Wing rooms and the remarkable Path of History tour. Travelers looking for contemporary luxury, however, should also consider Fairmont Hanoi, whose expansive rooms, modern take on Vietnamese design, and sprawling Cirua Spa make it the city's most exciting recent luxury arrival.

Is three days enough time to visit Hanoi?

In short, yes. Three days is enough to experience Hanoi's major highlights—including Hoàn Kiếm Lake, the Old Quarter, the French Quarter, and the Temple of Literature—plus the city's vibrant local food scene, while still leaving time to enjoy your hotel. If you're planning day trips to places like Ninh Bình or Hạ Long Bay (two to four hours away, respectively), I'd recommend adding at least one or two extra nights. After all, one of Hanoi's greatest pleasures is slowing down—lingering over classic egg coffee, wandering the ancient alleys, and spending an afternoon by the lake.

Every hotel review on this list has been written by a Condé Nast Traveler journalist who knows the destination and has visited that property. When choosing hotels, our editors consider properties across price points that offer an authentic and insider experience of a destination, keeping design, location, service, and sustainability credentials top of mind.

As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

#2970. Balikbayan Travel Checklist: Coming Home for Christmas 2026

At 6:22am on 23 December 2019, a traveler from Halifax, Canada, stepped out of NAIA and took the photo above: paper lanterns overhead, carts piled with bags, and a crowd so thick the security guard could barely see the road. That is the part of coming home everybody pictures. Nobody pictures the 40 minutes before it, at the baggage belt and the Customs lane, which is where most homecomings actually go wrong.

This balikbayan travel checklist is about those 40 minutes, and the four weeks before them. It is for OFWs flying home from the Gulf, Hong Kong, Singapore and Japan, and for Fil-Am and Fil-Can families bringing the kids home for Christmas 2026. We cover what to sort a month out, what your airline actually lets you carry, what Customs lets through duty-free, the one app every returning OFW needs before the trip back, and which NAIA terminal you land in now that the airlines have been shuffled around.

If you have not booked yet, read our guide on when to book Christmas flights from the Philippines first. This post picks up where that one stops.

Some links in this post are affiliate links. If you book through them we may earn a small commission at no extra cost to you, and it helps keep this site running. This is general travel information, not legal or immigration advice: rules change, so confirm with the agency named for your own situation.

Here is the whole thing on one screen, as of 8 October 2026.

The rest of this guide explains each line, with sources.

The cheapest Christmas seats are gone by October, and that is not news to anyone who has done this before. What is still in your control is how the trip is built.

Price the December premium honestly. On Trip.com’s Philippine site, checked 4 October 2026, Los Angeles to Manila averages PHP 99,210 return in December against PHP 57,908 in November and PHP 59,793 in January. Dubai to Manila averages PHP 47,691 return in December against PHP 38,969 in January. Those are whole-month averages, and the real squeeze sits between roughly 18 December and 4 January, so treat them as a floor, not a quote. If your leave allows a 10 December arrival or a mid-January return, that is where the savings are.

Compare flights home to Manila, Cebu and Clark on Trip.com

Put the domestic leg on the same ticket. If you are flying Riyadh to Manila and then Manila to Iloilo, book it as one itinerary. A delayed long-haul flight on one booking means the airline rebooks your connection. On two separate bookings, the second airline owes you nothing and your seat to Iloilo flies without you.

OFWs: get the OFW Pass before you fly home, not after. The paper Overseas Employment Certificate is gone for returning workers. The Department of Migrant Workers folded it into the OFW Pass, a free QR code in the eGovPH app, under DMW Advisory 38-2025 as reported by SunStar in December 2025. Open eGovPH, go to the DMW section and choose Balik-Manggagawa. If your contract is already in the DMW database the pass is issued automatically; if you changed employer or jobsite, update those first. The pass is valid for 90 days from issue, so time it so that it still covers your flight back to work.

It also matters on the way out of Manila. The OFW Pass is your proof for the travel tax and terminal fee exemption, which we explain in our guide to travel tax and terminal fees in the Philippines. Do it in your first week home, not on the morning of your flight, because processing can take a few working days when it is not automatic.

Every Christmas someone repacks a bag on the floor of Terminal 3 because they assumed 30 kg. The allowance is set by your fare, not your airline, and the spread is huge.

Here is what the airlines publish, as of 8 October 2026:

Where to look for your real number: your e-ticket receipt or the airline’s Manage Booking page, under baggage. Screenshot it. If the check-in agent and your receipt disagree, the screenshot ends the argument.

Two tips from the check-in line. Weigh every bag at home with a hand scale, because the 32 kg per-piece limit applies even when your total allowance is bigger. And split the heavy items: one bag of canned goods and one bag of clothes is how you end up paying excess on one and wasting space on the other.

If you are sending more than a suitcase can hold, we ran the per-kilo numbers on boxes, air cargo and extra bags in balikbayan box vs excess baggage vs cargo. Most sea cargo cutoffs for Christmas have already passed, so for 2026 the realistic options are a prepaid extra bag or air cargo.

The Bureau of Customs guidelines for arriving travelers come down to four numbers. Know them and the Customs lane is a walk-through.

Returning residents and foreigners have a similar PHP 150,000 cap on new personal items, limited to one of each kind. “One of each kind” is the line that catches people. One new phone for yourself is personal effects. Five new phones of the same model, still boxed, look like stock for a shop, and commercial quantities lose the exemption entirely.

Cash is a separate rule. You can carry up to PHP 50,000 in pesos; more needs written authorization from the Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas. Foreign currency above USD 10,000 (about PHP 628,000 at the BSP reference rate of PHP 62.80 on 2 October 2026) must be declared. Carrying it is not illegal; failing to declare it is the problem.

Pasalubong deserves its own post, and it is getting one. For now, the safe rule is personal quantities, original packaging and receipts in your hand-carry.

Register on eTravel within 72 hours of arrival. Every arriving passenger fills it in at etravel.gov.ph, and it carries your Customs baggage declaration and currency declaration, which replaced the old paper forms. It is free, so any site that asks for a card payment is not the government site. The QR code you get is for this trip only; do not show a saved one from last year.

Fil-Ams, Fil-Cans and Fil-Aussies: the balikbayan privilege is your best perk. Under Republic Act 9174, a former Filipino citizen gets visa-free entry for one year. So do a foreign spouse and children traveling with them. The Philippine Consulate in Frankfurt’s guidance says to tell the immigration officer you are availing of the privilege and to show your old Philippine passport or a copy of your Philippine birth certificate. Bring the marriage certificate for a spouse and birth certificates for the kids. We covered the spouse and children rules in detail in our post on the one-year visa-free balikbayan stay for foreign spouses and children.

The same law gives balikbayans a tax-exempt shopping allowance of USD 1,500 (about PHP 94,200) at government-operated duty-free shops. The usual window is 15 days from arrival, and the law stretches it to 30 days during the Christmas season, 15 November to 15 January. Duty Free Philippines has applied its own allowances over the years, so ask at the counter what it is honoring before you fill a cart.

One more thing for the US-based half of the family: American phone plans can charge heavily for roaming in the Philippines, and a Globe or Smart SIM means queuing at a mall kiosk on day one. An eSIM installed before you leave works the moment the wheels touch down. See Airalo’s Philippines eSIM plans and set it up at home while you still have Wi-Fi.

NAIA reshuffled its international airlines on 29 March 2026, and Japan Airlines followed on 1 April. Old blog posts and your tita’s memory are now wrong for several carriers. Per the list published by PhilSTAR Life on 31 March 2026:

Send that line to whoever is picking you up. Terminals 1 and 3 are not walking distance apart, and a driver circling the wrong one at midnight on 23 December is a bad start to the holidays.

Expect the queues. Last season NAIA handled 2.55 million passengers between 20 December 2025 and 5 January 2026, and the single busiest day was 20 December. This season’s forecast normally comes out in early December, so plan on last year’s pattern.

If you are connecting domestically. Landing on Emirates at Terminal 3 and flying PAL to Bacolod from Terminal 2 means collecting your bags, clearing Customs and changing terminals. Give that at least four hours on a peak date. A missed connection on separate tickets is the expensive Christmas mistake, which is a good reason to have cover: see what SafetyWing costs for your trip home, and read the delay and missed connection terms before you buy.

Getting out of the airport. If nobody is picking you up, a fixed-price car avoids the taxi line with three suitcases and a balikbayan box. Pre-book a fixed-price ride out of NAIA with Kiwitaxi. And OFWs heading out again in January should know the OFW lounge at NAIA Terminal 3, which makes the wait easier.

Landing late or flying on early the next morning? Sleeping near the airport beats a two-hour drive to the province at 1am. The map below shows rooms close to NAIA and what they cost per night.

For families in the Visayas and Mindanao, landing in Manila means a second December fare and a terminal change at the busiest airport in the country. Two options are worth checking this season.

Guam to Cebu, direct. Philippine Airlines is running seasonal PR121 flights from Guam to Cebu, departing 4:55am and landing at 6:30am, on 18, 24 and 25 December 2026 and 3, 4 and 7 January 2027, per the schedule filed with AeroRoutes on 15 September. That is aimed squarely at the Filipino community in Guam, and an early arrival in Mactan leaves the whole day for a ferry to Bohol or a bus up north. If your ferry is the next morning, compare rooms in Lapu-Lapu City near the airport.

Qatar into Clark. Qatar Airways flies to Clark, and its extra-baggage offer covers Clark as well as Manila. For families in Pampanga, Tarlac, Pangasinan or Nueva Ecija, Clark cuts out the drive through Metro Manila entirely. On Gulf routes, price Dubai to Manila fares against your Clark option on Trip.com before you assume Manila is cheaper, because once you add the bus or the private van north, it often is not.

No. For Balik-Manggagawa workers the OEC was replaced by the OFW Pass in the eGovPH app. It is free and valid for 90 days from issue.

Yes. eTravel is for all arriving passengers, Filipinos included, and must be done within 72 hours of arrival. It is free.

Goods worth PHP 10,000 or less are exempt for any traveler. Filipinos and OFWs can bring personal effects up to PHP 150,000 a year, across up to three shipments or arrivals, with boxes and baggage counted together.

Up to PHP 50,000 in pesos without Bangko Sentral authorization. Foreign currency above USD 10,000 must be declared on eTravel.

If you are a former Filipino citizen and he travels with you, yes, under the balikbayan privilege in RA 9174. Bring your old Philippine passport or birth certificate and your marriage certificate.

Terminal 3, along with Qatar Airways, Etihad, Gulf Air and Cebu Pacific. Saudia and Kuwait Airways use Terminal 1.

Coming home for Christmas is mostly paperwork you can do from your sofa in Dubai or San Diego. Get the OFW Pass early, screenshot your baggage allowance, keep pasalubong in personal quantities, register on eTravel three days out and tell your driver the right terminal. Do those five things and the 40 minutes between the plane and the arrivals barrier go quietly, which leaves the rest of the holiday for Simbang Gabi, Noche Buena and the family that has been counting down since September. Ingat sa biyahe, and maligayang pag-uwi.

She has been following Two Monkeys Travel Group since 2014, back when she was simply a reader dreaming, traveling, and collecting stories of her own, so being able to contribute to the blog now feels a little like coming full circle.

#2971. Scientists discover ghostly, eyeless creature hidden in Chinese cave

A newly identified freshwater cave snail in China is offering scientists a rare glimpse into the little-known aquatic ecosystems hidden beneath East Asia. Published in the journal Subterranean Biology, the discovery represents an important step forward in understanding the region's underground wildlife. Until now, no freshwater cave snails had been documented in China, and the country's subterranean freshwater mollusks had received relatively little scientific attention.

The newly discovered species, Erhaia dijiang, has evolved remarkable features that allow it to live in complete darkness. As a true cave-dwelling animal, it has entirely lost its eyes, with the structures normally associated with vision having fully degenerated. The tiny snail also has a cone-shaped, nearly transparent shell, a delicate body without pigmentation, and long, slender tentacles.

Its unusual appearance inspired a name drawn from ancient Chinese mythology. Researchers chose dijiang in honor of "Di Jiang," a mountain patron god portrayed as a faceless beast in the ancient text Classic of Mountains and Seas (山海经). The connection is particularly fitting because the snail is extremely sensitive to light. During laboratory observations, individuals consistently retreated into dark spaces beneath rocks whenever they were exposed to illumination.

The location of the discovery is just as remarkable as the animal itself. Scientists found Erhaia dijiang at an elevation of 2,062 meters, making it the highest-altitude cave snail yet recorded within the family Erhaiidae. The finding demonstrates that freshwater cave snails can inhabit limestone cave systems at elevations above 2,000 meters.

Life in the Dark Waters of a Mountain Cave

Within its underground habitat, Erhaia dijiang lives among submerged gravel and broken rock in water less than one meter deep. The cave's water temperature stays close to 20°C, providing a relatively stable environment for the tiny mollusks.

Even when heavy seasonal rains increase the flow of water and occasionally wash a few snails out through the cave opening, they have not been observed in nearby streams. Researchers found that the animals remain confined to the cave environment, staying within two meters of its entrance.

Laboratory observations also provided clues about what these elusive creatures eat. The snails were seen feeding on diatoms, microscopic algae that grow on rock surfaces, and they also consumed fish food supplied by the researchers.

Only Three Living Snails Found, Raising Conservation Concerns

Although the discovery sheds new light on China's largely unexplored underground aquatic biodiversity, researchers are concerned about the species' future. They describe its conservation outlook as "far from optimistic."

During the first field investigation in August 2025, the team encountered an exceptionally small number of snails. Despite searching the shallow cave waters, researchers located only three living adults and one empty shell. They found no juveniles, suggesting that the known population is extremely sparse.

The scientists are now urging more comprehensive investigations of cave ecosystems throughout China to uncover additional species and better understand these fragile underground habitats. They also emphasize the need to develop conservation protections quickly for these vulnerable mollusks, which are native to highly restricted environments and could face serious threats before their populations are fully understood.

Materials provided by Pensoft Publishers. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#2974. This is what the mysterious Denisovans looked like

Thank you for visiting nature.com. You are using a browser version with limited support for CSS. To obtain the best experience, we recommend you use a more up to date browser (or turn off compatibility mode in Internet Explorer). In the meantime, to ensure continued support, we are displaying the site without styles and JavaScript.

Rachel Fieldhouse is a reporter for Nature in Sydney, Australia.

Artist depiction of the heavy set facial features of a Denisovan.Credit: Institute of Vertebrate Paleontology and Paleoanthropology/Chinese Academy of Sciences

A low, sloping forehead, a massive brow ridge that curves backwards, rectangle-like eye sockets, flat cheeks and a moderately elevated bridge of the nose. Taken together, these features are the hallmarks of the ancient humans called the Denisovans, say researchers who studied a trove of fossilized skull fragments, teeth and jaws found in northern China.

“We are finally starting to put a face to the Denisovan,” says Sally Wasef, a palaeogeneticist at the Queensland University of Technology in Brisbane, Australia.

Denisovans were first described in 2010, after researchers sequenced genetic material from a mysterious finger bone discovered in a cave in Siberia and found that it did not resemble that of Homo sapiens or Neanderthals (Homo neanderthalensis). Ancient proteins have since been used to identify remains from other parts of Asia as Denisovan, including the first near-complete Denisovan skull found near Harbin, which helped to put a face on the ancient humans.

Researchers report in Nature1 today that they have an even better idea of what Denisovans looked like after analysing the physical features of remains that belong to a collection of 20 fossils found in Xujiayao.

The Xujiayao remains are at least 130,000 years old and were found in the 1970s, alongside animal remains and tools. At the time, it was unclear what type of human these fossils belonged to, and they were classified as a species called Homo juluensis. By analysing ancient proteins from some of the fossils, Qiaomei Fu, a geneticist at the Institute of Vertebrate Paleontology and Paleoanthropology in Beijing, and her team identified the remains as Denisovan.

Wasef says that ancient-protein analysis is a valuable tool to complement DNA studies, particularly because proteins can survive longer than can DNA without fully degrading. Although DNA provides more-detailed information about an individual, protein analysis can still yield useful information about who an individual is related to.

Fabrice Demeter, a palaeoanthropologist at the University of Copenhagen, says that the findings add to growing evidence that Denisovans were a widespread and potentially diverse group of humans. “The study represents a major step forward” in our understanding of what these humans could have looked like and where they lived.

Fu’s team analysed small fragments of proteins, called peptides, from two Xujiayao bones and compared them with peptides found in humans and Neanderthals. They found a single amino-acid change that was present in multiple peptides from the Xujiayao bones and other East Asian fossils and is a known genetic marker for identifying Denisovans. The amino-acid change was not seen in human or Neanderthal samples.

doi: https://doi.org/10.1038/d41586-026-03360-0

Clarification 21 October 2026: A previous version of this story used the word skulls instead of skull fragments.

Xing, S. et al. Nature https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-026-11132-z (2026).

#2975. 10 Animal Moves for Kids

Animal moves are great way to "trick" your child into exercising, building strength, improving mobility, and getting a great workout. The key is that rather than force exercise kids can have fun walking like a duck or instead of doing box jumps they can do frog jumps.

However, we are not quadrupeds or amphibians or water fowl—we are humans with completely different anatomies than the animals we mimic. Therefore our animal moves aren't exactly the same as the animals who actually perform them. They might look even different if you're an adult practicing animal movements for mobility and strength. For kids though, here are my ten favorite animal moves.

To perform the duck walk, sit in a full squat with heels down and feet angling out 8 to 15%. While staying in the squat position, walk. One cue to maintain proper technique is to ensure your head doesn’t bob up and down. Aim to keep your head level as you duck walk. This is an excellent exercise for quad strength and hip mobility.

A bear crawl is straightforward. Start on all fours and move on your hands and feet, keeping your back straight and parallel with the ground. Then, crawl contralaterally, which means moving your right arm with your left foot and your left arm with your right foot—opposite arm and leg moving together. As you become comfortable, try crawling forward, backward, to the right, and to the left. Challenge yourself further by crawling upstairs and downstairs or uphill both backward and forward.

This movement is an ideal general warm-up for an exercise day as it gets your shoulders firing and engages your core. One thing to consider is doing five minutes of bear crawls a day around your house. Just accumulate five minutes daily, and you will feel more limber than you ever thought possible.

Squat down on your heels and lightly place your hands on the floor in front of you for balance. Hop forward a foot or two without fully standing up, always maintaining that squatting position. Allow your hands to touch the floor, more as a reference point than as a base of support. Use this exercise to build strength in the squat's bottom position.

Begin in a position similar to the bunny hop, but with feet slightly wider and more weight on the hands. To move in the gorilla walk, place most of the weight on your arms and catapult your lower body forward. Imagine being a silverback gorilla vaulting through the jungle on your massive knuckles.

This is similar to the bunny hop, but there's no arm involvement. It's all about the quads and legs. Keep your torso more upright and hop along. You can also achieve a bit more hip extension on the jump, making it feel like more of a leap.

Lay down on your back and cross your arms. Move your hips and push off with your feet to crawl backward, pulling with your shoulder blades. This movement closely mimics a snake crawl. The key is to minimize limb use and rely mainly on your torso's gyration.

For the alligator crawl, start in a low plank position, as if you're at the bottom of a push-up, with weight on your hands and toes. Keep your belly and chest just above the ground and crawl. Start slowly, then try speeding up as you get the hang of it. This crawl might be one of the most challenging ones and is excellent for building isometric arm, tricep strength, and core tension.

Enjoying this post? Share it so others can find it!

The frog jump starts in a full squat. From there, jump as high and far as you can, landing back in a squat, then repeat. Ensure you maintain technical integrity; it's not a race. Each jump should represent maximal output with perfect technique. If you begin to feel tired and notice sloppy technique, stop the exercise. This jump is excellent for peak power output. If you continue frog jumps into your 40s, 50s, or even older, you'll be in a good position for long-term fitness.

There are a couple ways to do the monkey bars.

Bent arms: You can muscle your way across, keeping your arms flexed/bent as you pull yourself across from bar to bar. This is the fastest way to traverse the bars, but it's also very hard on your arms. A great bicep workout.

Straight arms: You can also swing with straight arms, using the momentum of your swinging legs and torso to carry you from bar to bar. This isn't about arm strength, rather it's predicated on being in rhythm and having a strong enough grip and stable shoulder girdle to support you going the distance.

Sit on the ground with your legs stretched out in front of you. From that position, place your hands and feet flat on the ground and lift yourself up so your body is no longer touching. Keeping your hips elevated, walk around forward, backward, and sideways. Always move contra laterally. Right hand, left foot. Left hand, right foot.

Kids can use animal moves at any point in the day, but if you're wondering when best to incorporate them take a look at the below suggestions:

A morning routine: Every morning, do 30 seconds to a minute of each of the animal moves. It's a great way to start the day and get some movement going and blood flowing.

Microworkouts: Every couple hours or so, do a minute or two of animal moves. Use them to break up sitting on the couch for extended amounts of time or better encourage them while kids are watching TV.

Warmups: Animal moves are great for warming up before a more intense activity. If your kid plays soccer duck walks, alligator walks, and frog jumps. If they play a racket sport try bear crawls or straight arm monkey bars. The point of a warmup is to increase body temperature, elevate the heart rate, and mobilize the targeted muscles and joints. Animal moves are great for all that.

Animal moves are a great way to have fun, build strength and mobility, and increase athleticism. Plus, they're not just for kids, they're great exercises for adults too.

Mark’s Daily Apple is still under construction. My team and I are actively working to repost the archives. Check back regularly to find posts that are new to you and revisit old favorites. Looking for a specific post? Let us know here.

#2976. Suburban Sprawl Puts Western Homes at Risk from Wildfires

This article was originally published by Grist and is reproduced here as part of the Climate Desk collaboration.

Homes burned in a recent wildfire in Spokane, Washington. U.S. Forest Service

Recent fires in Spokane, Washington, and Reno, Nevada, illustrate the risks from suburban sprawl, experts say. In both cities, encroaching flames burned homes built at the edge of wilderness.

Somersett, a short drive from downtown Reno, Nevada, is a peaceful place where homes with sweeping, high-desert views line cul-de-sacs and roads meander through shrubland, trailheads, and golf courses. But in the summer, months of low humidity and hot weather prime the landscape to burn. On the morning of August 22, it did.

In just a few days, strong winds carried the flames of the Hawk Fire more than 23 miles, placing some 90,000 people on evacuation notice as dense smoke and orange skies blanketed the city. By Wednesday, dozens of houses had been destroyed. Two weeks earlier, the Bug Fire burned 150 square miles just north of the same area.

It’s the latest in an era of worsening wildfires in the United States, where more than 7.9 million acres have burned this year. A similar story is playing out in Canada, where First Nations and residents of Ontario have borne the brunt of more than 5,100 fires across the country. Scientists say climate change has made these fires, along with others in Europe, significantly more likely to happen. This year’s blazes in the western U.S. were exacerbated by record-low winter snowfall, depriving the landscape of the moisture that typically seeps from snowpack throughout the summer, and by extreme and long-lasting heat waves that baked the landscape.

And the fires have highlighted another, deadly piece of the puzzle: suburban sprawl.

“What’s happened over the last decades is we’ve had explosive population growth in naturally fire-prone areas,” said Tim Brown, director of the Western Regional Climate Center at the Desert Research Institute in Nevada. “People are moving in to where fire happens.”

The wildland-urban interface, which researchers often call the WUI, is the phrase given to places where housing is sandwiched between paved city centers and forests or grasslands. Researchers have been raising the alarm about the fire risk posed to homes in these areas since the 1980s. As humans burn more fossil fuels and warm the planet, longer and more severe droughts will likely make that risk worse. Reno is the fastest warming city in the U.S., according to the nonprofit Climate Central.

These half-wild, half-suburban neighborhoods remain one of the most rapidly growing types of land in the country, although development has slowed compared to the surge between the 1990s and 2010s. In some places, housing scarcity in cities has pushed home buyers to the outskirts of towns, where building is generally cheaper and faces fewer permitting or zoning requirements.

People are also attracted to living with more space, near outdoor recreation areas with beautiful scenery. More than a third of all homes in the continental U.S. are now considered to be in the wildland-urban interface.

The consequences of all that growth are now on display. A recent study in Natural Hazards, a Nature journal, found that the expansion of this kind of housing in tandem with climate change quadrupled the risk of destructive wildfire in California. In January 2025, the Eaton and Palisades fires broke out in the shrubby sprawl surrounding Los Angeles, forcing more than 200,000 people to evacuate and burning down thousands of structures. Earlier this month, three large wildfires struck suburban areas of Spokane, Washington, the state’s second-biggest city.

“Maybe we need to stop calling it the WUI and call it what it is: fire-prone,” Brown said.

The Hawk Fire in Reno followed a familiar pattern. After the blaze started on Saturday morning in Somersett, gusty winds and dry, quick-to-light grasses helped it balloon by thousands of acres and quickly spread to other areas. The fire eventually shut down U.S. Route 395, a major thoroughfare that hugs part of the border between Nevada and California.

Chris Buckley, a public information officer with the U.S. Forest Service, said that the chances of a fire increase as more people move into one of these areas. In just the last 15 years, Reno’s population has grown by 26 percent to more than 283,000 people.

“It’s a very beautiful place to make your home,” Buckley said. “But during this type of weather in this part of the country, anything that can cause a spark is just so dangerous.” Like the vast majority of wildfires, she said, the Hawk Fire was human-caused.

Evacuating from neighborhoods in these fire-prone areas can be especially fraught. Roads are often narrow and connected by a single road with only one way out. During the recent wildfires in Spokane, the rush of fleeing traffic caused hourslong jams, leaving families sitting in their cars as the flames closed in. Similar scenes of gridlock unfolded during the 2018 Camp Fire in Paradise, California, and drives that normally took 25 minutes took hours.

“Our roads are not built for all people to leave all at once,” said Brett Taylor, who helps prepare these kinds of communities for wildfires with the Truckee Meadows Fire Protection District near Reno. Evacuating early, before mandatory orders, can help avoid gridlock and free up resources, he said. “We really lose our effectiveness if all we’re worrying about is evacuating people and not fighting the fire.”

The flames are just the start of the danger. As the planet warms, researchers estimate that wildfire smoke is on track to kill 70,000 Americans each year by 2050. When fires break out in cities like Reno or Spokane, the burning of cars and plastic objects, rather than just trees and grasses, can create smoke that is especially toxic. Studies estimate that the Los Angeles fires exposed millions of people to high concentrations of chromium-6, a cancer-causing toxic metal. “You can smell the difference in the smoke,” Taylor said.

Recently, California and other states have tightened wildfire-safety rules requiring homeowners in fire-prone areas to clear vegetation around their houses. Removing these plants can boost the chances that a house will survive a wildfire. The odds are even better for homes built with materials or techniques that prevent embers from getting in. “Many homes burn from the inside out,” Brown said.

In dry and hot places like Reno and Truckee, wildfire outbreaks are practically inevitable, Taylor said. He visits homes in the region to advise homeowners what changes they can make to better protect their property. His district is also spending millions of dollars from various state, federal, and other sources to thin or cut down trees and grasses — measures that can slow or block fires from spreading. “We’re making a dent,” he said. “But it’s a tiny fraction of what needs to be done.”

For America’s Rarest Wildlife, Survival May Hinge on a Single Word

        #2978. Bobsleigh Marble Run Challenge – Winter Olympics STEM

        Bobsleigh is one of the most exciting winter Olympic events. It’s fast, requires incredible precision on the turns and a huge amount of power to launch. This activity uses a marble run to explore the physics of bobsleighs!

        Bobsleigh athletes want to maintain as much energy as possible as they ride the track, so they try to lose as little energy to friction as they can. If the ice is soft or the blades on the bottom of the bobsleigh are rough there’s more friction between ths ice and sled slowing it down.

        Grab some marbles and some bits from the recycle bin and get ready to inspire the engineers ( and bobsleigh athletes ) of the future!

        First, design your marble run. You probably want a slope at the beginning to give the marble some initial speed, followed by twists and turns.

        As an extension task, add an extra challenge, for example, can you design a track that keeps a marble running for 60 seconds or longer?

        Another idea is to add bubble wrap or velcro to the track to increase the friction between the track and the marble, slowing it down.

        Try to get the marble run to move slowly, but with enough momentum to reach the end of the track.

        Gravity is an invisible force that pulls objects down to Earth. When a marble is at the top of a marble run, it has potential energy, when it starts to move the potential energy is converted to kinetic ( movement energy ) energy. At the top of the slope, the marble has 100% potential energy, but as it drops down, its potential energy goes down, and its kinetic energy goes up! The push start is really important in a bobsleigh race as the athletes want to add as much energy as they can at the top of the track.

        Rough surfaces slow moving objects down, so if you add bubble wrap or velcro to the inside of a tube, the marble will slow down. This is friction!

        Just a few of our marble run creations from over the years.

        Start simple and then add more complex features.

        Take a trial and error approach. Test sections of the track and then adjust as needed.

        Remember to think about gravity, friction and momentum.

        Learn about the science of the luge with my luge STEM Challenge.

        Make a cardboard tube bobsled with My Creative Life.

        #2979. 15 Best Hotels in the Dolomites, Italy's Charming Mountain Region

        In winter, a snowglobe scene of dusted pine forests and glittering frozen lakes surrounds the best hotels in the Dolomites. It thaws just in time for summer’s “the hills are alive” buttercup meadows and lush valleys, all surrounded by perfect peaks. Indeed, the Dolomites mountain range is maddeningly beautiful, a cinematic landscape dotted with storybook chalet towns and villages—it's just one reason the region received its moment in the spotlight during 2026’s Winter Olympics.

        Visitors will notice the Dolomites remain deeply rooted in Ladin culture and traditions, with Roman and Milanese weekenders setting a palpably Italian (not touristy) tone despite the area's rising popularity. Ready to stay and see the fairy tale with your own eyes? From restrained eco-boutiques with easy access to the slopes to spa stalwarts with steaming outdoor pools tipping over pastoral valleys, the best hotels in the Dolomites offer year-round appeal. Read on for our editors' favorites.

        Nature dictates everything at this CO2-neutral A-list hideaway in the Italian Dolomites. The site was chosen for its abundant sunshine, spring water, mountain air, and views of the jagged Odle massif, which is framed by picture windows in all 62 minimalist suites. The landscape is also woven into the biophilic architecture, Celtic wellness rituals, and intricate “forest cuisine.” After hiking or skiing in the Plose mountains, guests can recharge over a tasting menu in the amphitheater restaurant as the sun sets with all the drama of Mount Doom. The history of the complex is similarly dramatic: It was conceived in 1912 as a sanatorium for Austro-Hungarian aristocrats but lay dormant for years. For its relaunch in 2020, Stefan and Teresa Hinteregger added three 12-story towers and an astonishing spa with a swim-in, swim-out pool. A villa for 10 followed in December 2024, and a concept restaurant, Yera, which celebrates the fruits of the forest around a firepit in a cave, arrived in June 2025. —Lisa Johnson

        This cliffside-hugging, glass-fronted forest retreat has an Instagram-famous pool that’s framed by the Dolomites’ famous peaks. But better yet, it’s a beautiful hotel designed to celebrate the region's shifting seasons. The mix of guests ranges from trendy couples lounging in dressing gowns on a spa break to outdoorsy types wearing messy boots and young families with grandparents in tow on the hunt for some truly fresh air. Book a guided forest bathing walk to meander slowly through the woods and fully immerse yourself in nature.

        Back at the hotel, the heated onsen pool etched into the cliff face is the perfect spot to relax, while the larger infinity pool is made for taking in sweeping views of the valley below. There are 47 rooms in six different categories, all of which face jaw-dropping mountain views. Cozier rooms in the main house sleep up to four and benefit from easy access to the pool, restaurants, and bar. Alternatively, there are three houses 985 feet away. I was driven to one in a Miramonti-branded vehicle, where I entered through our own garage. A private elevator opens to reveal an open-plan living space, interiors crafted from natural materials, and light-wood walls that still emit their earthy scent. Bespoke, custom-made furniture and decor pepper the space and prove the precision is in the details. —Sophie Knight

        Located in the heart of the Dolomites on the Alpe di Siusi—the highest Alpine plateau in Europe—the ski-in, ski-out location offers direct access to the pistes in the winter, and South Tyrol’s flower-flecked network of hiking and biking trails in the summer. As you’d expect from the brand, wellness is at its core, with a health-forward treatment menu on offer, plus hearty Tyrolean and Italian fare—as well as excellent Alto Adige wines—for those who don’t want to hold back. All of the wood-clad, cozy rooms face the slopes and have a balcony or terrace. Sauna is a serious operation here, with herbal and Finnish saunas, salt and aroma steams, and a convivial outdoor seating area in the snow where half-clad spa-goers chatter. In summer, the hotel offers multiple activities including biking, hiking, climbing, trail running, paragliding, zip-lining, wild swimming, and helicopter rides. —Jemima Sissons

        Set high above the South Tyrolean valley, Hotel Saltus is a design-led sanctuary immersed in forest and mountain views. This family-run retreat lives and breathes its surroundings, with chic contemporary minimalism that lets the views do the talking. Rooms are calm, expansive, and built to frame the ever-changing landscape, while the food celebrates local ingredients with a refined, modern twist. Wellness is at the heart of the experience, from the serene Forest Spa to guided forest bathing sessions that encourage guests to slow down and reconnect, both with themselves and with the earth. Whether you’re here to hike through the breathtaking Dolomites or simply switch off, Saltus offers a deeply restorative stay where time stretches and allows you to lean into the art of doing nothing. I expected to return home feeling more relaxed, but I was surprised to find myself returning with a little bit of Hotel Saltus lodged in my heart forever. —Lucy Bruton

        Of all the chic new hotels that have opened in the Dolomites in recent years, Castel Badia—set in a manor house that dates back to the year 1000—is at the top of the pile. The hotel, an Alpine counterpart to ­Puglia’s Borgo Egnazia, was created by Ian Schrager’s protégé Aldo Melpignano, who brings a new sense of cool to high-end Italian hospitality. The building has served as a courthouse, a convent, a private castle, and, since the 1970s, a hotel. A recent renovation saved everything that could be saved: The floors, wood ceilings, stuccoed vaults, and frescoes remain. Silence reigns in this ancient castle, where life, as it did in the Middle Ages, revolves around the courtyard. Through that nexus, you access the garden, swimming pool, guest rooms (there are 28 in total, along with a three-story chalet), basement spa with frescoed relaxation rooms overlooking the mountains, and the crypt of San Lorenzo, a protected site that, like the rest of the castle, is open to the public. This remains a sacred place, even as guests enjoy its sybaritic offerings. —Sara Magro

        At the foot of the Alpe di Siusi, the highest alpine pasture in Europe, Sensoria Dolomites sits low, nestled beside a river in a South Tyrolean village. This is an adults-only all-inclusive retreat where rooms are minimalist, making way for maximalist views via large windows and wraparound balconies. Days ideally begin with a dip in the heated pool, with steam rising as the Dolomite peaks sharpen into view, or with a relaxing sound meditation in the spa before breakfast at Anima restaurant. From here, it’s a five-minute stroll to the cable car. In summer, a short ascent delivers you straight onto rolling alpine pasture; in winter, locals wander aboard with skis slung casually over their shoulders, bound for the wide, sunlit slopes. —Noo Saro-Wiwa

        Adler Balance was built in 2008 right next to its sister property, Adler Dolomiti, and the structure was created to blend harmoniously into the Alpine landscape. Unlike Adler Dolomiti, Adler Balance is adults-only and has a particular focus on wellness. The hotel has evolved into a highly specialized destination medical spa, combining a five-star hotel experience with longevity programs led by a team of doctors, therapists, and wellness experts. Nestled in a peaceful corner of the Dolomites, it offers a retreat from the crowds that have started to flock here year-round. Large and comfortable rooms, attentive staff, and an elegant dining scene are all present, but it's the hotel's mountain heritage, as well as its picture-perfect location, that's the main event. —Alessia Armenise

        Nestled in the Lüsen valley at the foot of the Dolomites, Lüsnerhof Naturhotel is the kind of place you’d let your mind wander to when dreaming of peace and tranquility. Escaping the city for a weekend here feels close to medicinal. The well-being offering is bespoke and holistic, championing regional produce as well as the skills of specialist practitioners who visit for a week at a time to share their craft. Each of the hotel's 59 rooms is cleverly designed to optimize its surroundings and spotlight the location, no matter the season. Interiors reflect the Alpine setting and integrate years-old Dolomite rock, Swiss stone pine, and a clay finish on the walls. Fresh mountain water awaits guests in their rooms—a small but location-centric touch. The spa here is huge, and home to the longest sauna trail in the Alps. Practices take place in immersive outdoor locations, including the forest sauna, floating salt grotto, natural bathing pond, whirlpools, and infrared gondola. —Alice Morey

        During my three-hour drive from Milan to Madonna di Campiglio, the landscape shifted from Call Me By Your Name to a real-life vintage ski poster. Adamello Brenta Nature Park, which surrounds the area, was beautiful enough to spark emotions as we drove past tumbling waterfalls and turquoise lakes, where the mountains’ reflections are as awe-inspiring as the peaks themselves. When we pulled up to Casa Cook Madonna, we were delighted to see our neighbors were a chocolatier, a local cheese shop, an ancient little stone church, and the base of the gondola, from where cable cars serenely floated up the mountain, which we watched from our room’s reading nook. Each of the 50 rooms can be summed up as contemporary, cozy, and intimate, and modern in a way that truly magnifies the surrounding environment. Think earthy stone surfaces, deep woods, and sleek modern headboards juxtaposed with the kind of rustic, wooly blankets that would fit nicely into Ralph Lauren’s Colorado ranch. Being at the base of the slopes, you’re in a prime position to ski. —Tamara Southward

        It's hard not to be awestruck when you catch your first glimpse of this hotel—it resembles a magnificent spaceship, its stark rectangular silhouette appearing to levitate above the treetops, illuminated from beneath and casting an enchanting glow that creates the illusion of hovering. The vision is the work of owner Markus Huber, a native of Brixen, and Gerhard Tauber, who designed the contemporary tree house concept, the largest of its kind in Europe. Perched on lofty 115-foot stilts resembling tree trunks, My Arbor—which fittingly means “my tree” in Latin—is a tree house that's strictly for grown-ups, attracting a health-conscious, sociable, and energetic crowd seeking adventure and relaxation in equal measure. The location is equally a knockout, set in stunning surroundings with direct access to the Plose ski area from right outside the hotel. After days out hiking or on the slopes, the spa is the ultimate relaxation spot, spanning 27,000 square feet over two floors, and comprising saunas, a Turkish steam room, an icy plunge pool, and a large panoramic outdoor hot tub with incredible views over the valley. —Karin Mueller

        You can sense a unique vibe at San Sonnea Family Dolomites—a family-friendly wellness, farm, and ski hotel—when you first arrive. Perhaps it’s down to the mutual understanding shared by all the adults-with-hangers-on here. It's a place for parents who want to enjoy the finer things and locations in life, no matter how many boundary-pushing blow-ups their toddlers or teens may have in store, such as the slopes of the Dolomites, organic fine dining and drinking, and luxurious but eco-friendly design and decor. Fortunately, the many creative activities on offer in the phenomenal kids and babies clubs are likely to stop oncoming tantrums in their tracks, from pony rides and alpaca petting to finding their own breakfast eggs at the little farm and taking part in productions in the huge theater. Access to a 2,900-foot-long slope and lift comes included, with private ski lessons easily arranged for those ages two-and-a-half and up. Besides the food and drinks, the property's very highest highlight is its five pools, including a shallow option with a cinema screen, one with speedy slides, and an indoor-outdoor heated pool from which steam wafts, ideally situated for swimmers to marvel at the snow-capped mountains nearby, or firepit come nightfall. —Becky Lucas

        Since 1939, Rosa Alpina has been a firm favorite with the high-altitude summer crowd for its Alpine classicism and San Cassiano location. Then Aman took the mantle in 2003, and its Ladin roots (once a farm and inn) were thrust through the group’s contemporary prism: the hallmark clean lines, subdued hues, and angular furniture. Rather than end three generations of stewardship, Aman kept the Pizzinini family firmly at the helm, respecting their hotelier pedigree and, ultimately, preserving something no amount of glass, steel, blonde woods or Michelin stars can buy: soul. Long-time Aman collaborator, Jean-Michel Gathy, rolled up his sleeves and integrated the notion of Alpine coziness into Aman’s famously restrained aesthetic with clever lighting, milky fabrics reflecting the surrounding pine forests and snow-capped peaks, and abundant cubic fireplaces warming the pared-down rooms.

        Guests can paraglide, whoosh down the endless runs forming the Dolomiti Superski area, gallop through the fragrant pine forests on horseback, or simply take a remedial walk through the UNESCO-listed landscape with its crisp, lung-cleansing air. And with Aman’s finely tuned spa and excellent food at The Grill, there’s always something worth trudging back to base for. —Maddalena Fossati

        To soak up illustrious glamour, head to the pearl of the Dolomites, Cortina d’Ampezzo, where you’re likely to splurge most of your budget on oysters and Champagne. But there’s a reason why this northern gem, located only two hours away from Venice, has become a favorite among Italians and was the host of the 2026 Winter Olympics. Around the area, you’ll find a handful of beautiful lakes, including Lago di Misurina and, slightly further, Lago di Braies. The perfect base for your adventures is Rosapetra, whose secluded location on the outskirts of Cortina offers a low-key, relaxing few days. Rosapetra is an Alpine cottage where each of the 33 rooms looks out towards the Dolomites. From a well-equipped gym to a wellness circuit of iced waterfalls, saunas, and a 39-foot swimming pool, you’ll have everything you need to warm up or restore your muscles pre- and post-ski. —Lucrezia Worthington

        Among the remarkable landscapes of the Val Gardena lies Hotel Tyrol, a family-run Alpine dwelling acting as both a home to its owners and a hotel for guests. Tradition and warmth are the essence of this place, with espresso-colored wood enhanced with bright velvet sofas and decor objects kept since the hotel's first opening in 1966. All 50 rooms draw inspiration from Ladin architecture, integrating spruce wood furnishings with the warmth of a Tyrolese stove. Enjoy dinner at Michelin-starred Ristorante Suinsom, where chef Alessandro Martellini enriches your palate with a take on traditional Ladin recipes in a five- or six-course tasting menu. Alternatively, book a table at TyRestaurant for a relaxed dining approach. From stepping in and out of the various saunas to scheduling a spa ritual (at the end of the “Alpine Dream,” the team serves hot chocolate and homemade cakes), you’ll return home feeling energized.

        How we choose the best hotels in the Dolomites

        Every hotel on this list has been selected independently by our editors and written by a Condé Nast Traveler journalist who knows the destination and has visited that property. When choosing hotels, our editors consider properties across price points that offer an authentic and insider experience of a destination, keeping design, location, service, and sustainability credentials top of mind.

        This gallery has been updated with new information since its original publish date. It was originally published on Condé Nast Traveller UK.

        As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.
