        #2980. Osaka on a Filipino Budget: Five Days With Real Numbers

        The cheapest return seat from Manila to Osaka was PHP 18,438 when we checked, on Cebu Pacific for 17 to 19 November. A dorm bed in Namba can cost under PHP 1,200 a night. A bowl of Sukiya gyudon is ¥480, about PHP 191. And still, plenty of first-timers come home from Osaka having spent far more than they planned.

        The gap is never the ramen. It is the stuff nobody puts in the budget: the travel tax at NAIA, the airport train, Japan’s new ¥3,000 departure tax, and the tax-free shopping rules that change on 1 November 2026.

        This is an Osaka budget travel guide for Filipinos: five days, built from prices we checked in early October 2026, with every yen figure converted to pesos. It covers the visa, flights from Manila, Cebu and Clark, where to sleep, what to eat, the day trips to Kyoto and Nara, and two full budget tables you can copy into your own spreadsheet. We used ¥1 = PHP 0.397, the European Central Bank reference rate on 2 October 2026, and rounded to the nearest peso.

        Some links in this guide are affiliate links. If you book through them we may earn a small commission at no extra cost to you, and it never changes what we recommend.

        Five days and four nights in Osaka costs a Filipino traveler about PHP 37,000 per person on a tipid budget and about PHP 45,000 on a comfortable one, flights included, as of October 2026. Here is what sits under those totals:

        If you only remember one thing: Osaka itself is cheap. Getting there and the add-ons are what move the total.

        You need a visa. There is no visa-free entry to Japan for ordinary Philippine passports as of October 2026.

        The good news is the price. Japan raised its visa fees for many nationalities from 1 July 2026, with a single-entry visa at about ¥15,000, but the Embassy of Japan said the temporary visitor visa for Philippine nationals remains gratis. What you do pay is the service fee at the Japan Visa Application Centre run by VFS Global. The VFS fee sheet lists PHP 520, while a June 2026 Philippine News Agency report put it at PHP 800, so budget PHP 520 to 800 and confirm the amount when you book your appointment.

        Since 7 April 2025, applications go through the JVAC branches in Parañaque, Makati, Quezon City, Cebu and Davao, not through accredited travel agencies. Processing is at least six business days in Manila and ten in Cebu or Davao, and you can apply no earlier than 90 days before your flight. Book the appointment early in peak months, because slots fill up weeks ahead.

        There is one shortcut worth knowing. Since December 2025, Filipinos joining a package tour from one of ten registered Philippine agencies can travel on an eVISA, with a stay of up to 15 days. It suits first-timers and older parents, but you lose the freedom to build your own route, so compare the package price against the DIY totals below.

        We cover the full document list, bank certificate rules and refusal reasons in our Japan visa guide for Filipinos. Sort the visa before you buy a non-refundable fare.

        Osaka is one of the easiest Japan trips from the Philippines because three airports fly there nonstop. These are the fares Trip.com was showing in late September 2026 for Kansai International Airport:

        Two warnings before you get excited. First, those are base fares. On Cebu Pacific and Jetstar the cheapest bundle has no checked bag, and a Japan trip almost always comes home heavier. Our breakdown of what a piso fare really costs once bags and fees are added applies here too.

        Second, timing. On the Manila route, Trip.com’s average return fare over the past 12 months was lowest in September at PHP 20,519 and highest in December at PHP 34,240. One-way averages peak in April, which is cherry blossom season. If you have flexible leave, a November trip lands between Undas and the Christmas rush and still catches the autumn colors in late November. Our guide to the best time to book flights from the Philippines explains how far ahead to lock it in.

        Check current Manila to Osaka fares on Trip.com

        From KIX to the city: the regular Nankai Airport Express to Namba is ¥970 (about PHP 385) and takes about 45 minutes. The rapi:t limited express is faster at 34 minutes, but the reserved seat adds ¥700, so you pay about ¥1,670 (PHP 663). For a budget trip, take the regular train. If you are staying near Shin-Osaka or Umeda instead, JR West sells a discounted Haruka one-way ticket to foreign visitors for ¥1,800 to Osaka or Shin-Osaka.

        The neighborhood matters more than the hotel. Stay near a station on the Midosuji subway line and every day trip in this plan starts with a short walk.

        Namba and Nipponbashi is the default for a first trip. Nankai trains from KIX end here, Dotonbori is a walk away, and Kintetsu trains to Nara leave from Osaka-Namba. Shin-Imamiya and Shinsekai is the cheapest area in central Osaka, one stop from Namba, with older budget inns and the kushikatsu stalls under Tsutenkaku tower. Umeda suits you if Kyoto is your priority, since Hankyu and JR trains to Kyoto leave from here.

        These are ranges from 2026 price surveys, not quotes, and they climb on weekends, holidays and during the autumn leaves.

        Then there is the accommodation tax, which Osaka Prefecture raised on 1 September 2025. It is charged per person per night, based on what each person pays for the room:

        Here is the tipid trick hidden in that table. A ¥9,000 business hotel room shared by two is ¥4,500 each, which falls under the ¥5,000 line. Two kababayans sharing a room pay no tax at all, while a solo traveler in the same room pays ¥200 a night.

        Compare budget rooms in Namba near Nankai and Kintetsu stations, or browse the live map below. Prices on the map are nightly rates.

        Osaka calls itself the kitchen of Japan, and the cheap food is the good food. You can eat well on about ¥2,800 a day (PHP 1,112) if breakfast comes from the konbini.

        Kuromon Market is worth one morning, but it is priced for tourists now. Expect ¥400 to ¥800 for a single grilled scallop and ¥800 to ¥1,500 for a wagyu skewer. Treat it as your one splurge meal, not your daily breakfast.

        For a proper sit-down list, our Dotonbori food guide names the restaurants worth queueing for. And if you miss home, the cheap set meals at Sukiya and Matsuya are as close to a rice-and-ulam lunch as Japan gets.

        This plan uses Osaka as your base the whole time. One hotel, no packing and unpacking, and Kyoto and Nara are both under an hour away. Our older 3-day Osaka itinerary has more on the sights inside the city if you want to swap days around.

        Day 1: Arrive and walk Dotonbori. Nankai to Namba (¥970), drop your bags, and spend the evening on the canal under the Glico sign. Before you leave the airport, buy an ICOCA card at the JR ticket office in Kansai-airport Station: ¥2,000, which includes a ¥500 deposit and ¥1,500 of fare. Foreign visitors need their passport to buy one.

        Day 2: The Osaka Amazing Pass day. The one-day pass costs ¥3,500 (PHP 1,390) for sales from 25 March 2026 to 31 March 2027, and it is digital only. It covers Osaka Metro and city buses plus free entry to more than 40 places. Do the maths before buying. Osaka Castle Main Tower is ¥1,200 since April 2025, and the Umeda Sky Building observatory is ¥2,000, free with the pass until 3pm. Those two alone are ¥3,200, and add a ride on the Tombori River Cruise and a day of subway trips, and the pass has paid for itself. Start at the castle when it opens and reach Umeda before 3pm. Buy the Osaka Amazing Pass on Klook, where the one-day pass was about PHP 1,284 when we checked, slightly below the official yen price.

        Day 3: Kyoto. JR special rapid from Osaka to Kyoto is ¥580 each way (PHP 230) and takes about 30 minutes. Hankyu from Osaka-umeda to Kyoto-kawaramachi is cheaper at ¥410 but slower. Fushimi Inari is free and open around the clock, so go at sunrise before the crowds, then Kiyomizu-dera (¥500).

        Day 4: Nara. Kintetsu from Osaka-Namba to Kintetsu-Nara is ¥680 each way. The deer crackers are ¥200 for a bundle of ten, and Todai-ji’s Great Buddha Hall is ¥800. Bow to the deer and they often bow back. Hold the crackers high, because they will also headbutt you for them. Back in Osaka, do dinner in Shinsekai, where kushikatsu is cheap and the tower is lit up.

        Day 5: Pasalubong and fly home. Morning at Kuromon, then Don Quijote for the pasalubong run, then Nankai back to KIX. Leave enough time for the new tax-free refund counter, explained below.

        Want Universal Studios instead? Swap Day 4. The one-day Studio Pass starts at ¥8,400 (PHP 3,335) for visits from 1 September 2026 and rises with demand, so the earlier you buy, the cheaper it tends to be. Check Universal Studios Japan tickets on Klook for your date.

        Per person, five days and four nights, as of October 2026. Yen at PHP 0.397.

        Tipid plan: dorm bed, konbini breakfasts, gyudon and conveyor sushi.

        Comfortable plan: shared business hotel room, a sit-down dinner every night.

        Not included in either: checked baggage on a promo fare, Universal Studios (add PHP 3,335 or more), pasalubong, and the ¥500 ICOCA deposit, which you get back. The ¥3,000 departure tax is also not listed separately, because airlines collect it in the ticket price.

        The tax-free change on 1 November 2026. Until now, stores took the 10% consumption tax off at the counter. From 1 November 2026, you pay the full price, show your passport at the till, and claim the refund at customs when you leave Japan, before you check in for your flight. Keep your purchases and receipts together until you reach the airport, and give yourself extra time at KIX, since a customs queue now sits between you and check-in. Consumables you eat in Japan are not refundable. The rule changes the timing, not the savings, so do not let a cashier convince you the discount is gone.

        Connectivity. Japan’s free wifi is patchy outside stations. Airalo’s Japan eSIM had unlimited plans from $17 for five days (about PHP 1,065) when we checked. See Airalo’s Japan eSIM plans and install it before you leave Manila, so it works the moment you land.

        Insurance. Travel insurance is not a requirement for the Japan tourist visa, but a hospital visit in Japan is expensive for anyone paying in pesos. See what SafetyWing costs for a short Japan trip and decide for yourself.

        Filipinos in Osaka. The Philippine Consulate General in Osaka is at Twin 21 MID Tower, 24th floor, in Chuo-ku. Save its emergency number, 090-4036-7984, in your phone before you fly. For Sunday Mass in English, Osaka Umeda Catholic Church has services at 9:00 and 13:00. A church directory lists a monthly Tagalog Mass at Tamatsukuri Cathedral, but we could not confirm it with the Archdiocese, so ask the parish before you go.

        Cash. Osaka is far more card-friendly than it was, but small ramen shops, temple stalls and market vendors still prefer yen. 7-Eleven ATMs take international Visa and Mastercard cards, which covers most Philippine debit cards.

        How much pocket money do I need for 5 days in Osaka? About ¥40,000 (PHP 15,700) per person on a tipid plan, covering a dorm bed, food, trains and entry fees. Plan for ¥55,000 (PHP 21,600) if you share a hotel room and eat out properly.

        Is Osaka cheaper than Tokyo for Filipinos? It depends more on your hotel than on the city. Osaka’s edge is that Kyoto and Nara are cheap day trips from one base, so you see three cities on one room. Check fares to both, since they move separately.

        Do Filipinos need a visa for Osaka? Yes. Japan requires a visa for Philippine passport holders. The visa itself is free; you pay only the JVAC service fee.

        Is the Osaka Amazing Pass worth it? Only on a day you visit Osaka Castle and the Umeda Sky Building before 3pm. Those two cost ¥3,200 on their own, against ¥3,500 for the pass.

        When is the cheapest time to go to Osaka from Manila? Trip.com’s 12-month data shows the cheapest average return fares in September and the highest in December.

        Osaka rewards the budget traveler more than almost anywhere in Japan. The street food is the real food, the best day trips cost under PHP 600 in train fare, and one hotel in Namba covers the whole trip.

        Lock in the visa appointment first, then the fare, then the room. Build in the travel tax, a checked bag if you shop, and an extra half hour at KIX for the new tax refund. Do that, and PHP 37,000 gets you five honest days in Osaka with real food and no surprise bills when you get home.

        #2982. As Death Toll From Nepal Flood Rises, Officials Call to Tackle Warming

        Security camera footage of a massive flood hitting Gyirong Port at the border of China and Nepal on August 26. Wikipedia

        Five days after a calamitous flood washed through Rasuwa, Nepal, the death toll continues to climb. Authorities said Monday that 903 people have been confirmed dead, while another 4,247 are missing, as the country reels from a disaster that experts say may be linked to the melting of glacial ice.

        Officials believe the collapse of a glacier on Langtang Lirung peak sent an avalanche of ice and rock careening into a valley below, where it blocked the flow of the Trishuli River. When the rock barrier finally gave way, they say, it unleashed a rush of water and mud several stories high. The floodwaters inundated villages all along the river, not just in Nepal but also in neighboring Tibet, where 16 people are confirmed dead and another 546 are still missing, according to Chinese state media.

        Researchers with the HiRisk project, which tracks threats from melting ice in the Himalayas, say that the shrinking and thawing of glaciers may have fueled the disaster. Over the last three decades, Nepali mountains have lost roughly a third of their ice, according to the U.N. Between 1990 and 2020, the glacier on Langtang Lirung retreated roughly 1,500 feet, and experts say the loss of ice may have left the slope more vulnerable to a landslide.

        Further upping the risk of collapse was a warm spell that melted snow atop Langtang Lirung in the days before the flood. “That snowmelt had to go somewhere – and it may have supplied the water that lubricated the rockfall,” said Umesh Haritashya, a geoscientist at the University of Dayton.

        The upper Himalayas are warming nearly twice as fast as the global average, and August is the warmest time of year in the region. “While scientists are still studying the exact causes of this disaster, it is exactly the type of event that is triggered by climate change,” said Wouter Buytaert, a hydrologist at Imperial College London.

        U.N. climate chief Simon Stiell said in a statement that a warming climate “is making tragedies like this one, and so many others around the world, much more likely, frequent, and severe, with vulnerable communities often hardest hit.”

        In a press conference on Saturday, Nepal’s foreign minister, Shisir Khanal, called on countries to address warming. “We’d ask the world,” he said, “to take climate change as one of the major challenges that Nepal and its citizens face, and this has to be a global effort.”

        #2983. High-Antioxidant Foods

        We're all supposed to eat a “healthy diet,” but what does that really mean? Off the bat, you probably think getting enough about protein and fatty acids (notably the vital omega-3s), plus important vitamins and minerals. But there’s also the less obvious: things like prebiotics and, the topic of today’s post, antioxidants.

        Antioxidants, as I’ve discussed previously, defend the body from free radical damage. Free radicals are unstable molecules that knock around, pillaging electrons from other molecules and causing DNA damage and oxidative stress. When free radicals run amok, the damage they cause contributes to premature aging and chronic diseases. Antioxidants’ job is to limit free radical formation and neutralize them when their numbers become too great.

        Plant and animal foods deliver important antioxidant vitamins, minerals, flavonoids, carotenoids, and other substances that the body can’t produce itself. To cover your antioxidant bases, since there’s no way to avoid free radicals, here are some antioxidant-rich foods to include in your diet.

        Certain antioxidants are fairly easy to quantify. You can use any food tracking app to look up the amounts of antioxidant vitamins A, C, and E you consume in a day. Others, like antioxidant enzymes, are considerably more complicated. Scientists have devised various methods of measuring the total antioxidant capacity of food. You have your crocin bleaching assay (CBA), trolox equivalent antioxidant capacity (TEAC), total oxyradical scavenging capacity assay (TOSC), ferric reducing antioxidant potential (FRAP), DPPH assay, and many more.1

        Don’t worry, there won’t be a quiz later.

        ORAC, or oxygen radical absorbance capacity, was developed by scientists at the National Institute on Aging in 1992. Despite its popularity, the USDA renounced ORAC in 2012, on the grounds that ORAC doesn't necessarily predict how much antioxidant activity happens in the body and to discourage supplement manufacturers from making strong claims about the power of their antioxidant formulations.

        Nevertheless, researchers continue to use ORAC, and it remains the best way for the average person to look up which foods have relatively higher or lower antioxidant content.

        The question of whether these foods actually supercharge your body's ability to fight free radicals is still up for debate. A lot of factors probably go into it, including how much of these compounds you absorb and the freshness of the food you consume.

        As I see it, the best reason to consider antioxidant values is that you’ll probably end up revisiting some otherwise nutritious foods you might not be eating regularly.

        The foods that tend to score highly on antioxidant metrics are also ones that are widely considered healthy by other standards—colorful vegetables and fruits, organ meat, and seafood.

        Plant foods tend to be higher in antioxidants than animal products, though the latter are still important for checking certain antioxidant boxes. “Eating the rainbow” will net you a good spectrum of vitamins, minerals, and other antioxidants. You can also intentionally add high-antioxidant foods in the mix.

        Here are 12 plant-based foods and beverages that can boost your antioxidant intake (based on a combination of ORAC and FRAP scores):

        If you're lucky enough to have dog rose berries or rowanberry in your area, definitely include those as well.

        On an absolute basis, the antioxidant capacity of these foods is lower than the plant foods above. However, these animal foods are among the best sources of the trace minerals copper, selenium, iron, manganese, and zinc, plus other antioxidants like lutein that are crucial pieces of the body’s antioxidant defense system.

        What say you? Do you think about antioxidants when putting together your meal plan, or is that not really a consideration for you? What are your favorite high-antioxidant foods?

        https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8347950/

        #2984. Hurricane warning maps are improving, but a dangerous problem remains

        An updated hurricane forecast map is making it easier for people to identify areas threatened by storms, but new research reveals a concerning limitation. Even when people can recognize where hurricane watches are in effect, they may not feel more at risk or become more inclined to take precautions.

        Researchers at Florida State University examined whether changes to the National Hurricane Center (NHC) forecast graphics improve public understanding of hurricane threats. The study involved faculty from the School of Communication within the College of Communication and Information and the Department of Geography.

        The researchers evaluated an experimental graphic that expands the traditional hurricane forecast cone to include tropical storm and hurricane watches and warnings for inland areas. The familiar cone has historically placed greater emphasis on coastal regions, even though dangerous storm conditions can extend far from the shoreline.

        The findings revealed mixed results. Although the revised graphic helped people without specialized weather knowledge recognize inland areas under hurricane or tropical storm watches, it did not significantly influence how dangerous they believed the storms would be or whether they intended to take protective measures. Participants also showed no improvement in identifying areas covered by storm warnings.

        The updated format was developed by the NHC in 2024, and the findings were published in the American Meteorological Society journal Weather, Climate, and Society.

        "The National Hurricane Center has made a number of thoughtful changes to improve how hurricane information is communicated," said Assistant Professor Elizabeth Ray, the study's lead author. "We had a simple question. Do those changes actually help people understand what the graphic is telling them about the risk they face?"

        Why People Misunderstand Hurricane Forecast Maps

        To investigate how the public interprets hurricane forecasts, the research team surveyed residents of Florida and Georgia. Participants were shown either the conventional hurricane cone graphic or the newer experimental version that includes additional information about inland storm threats.

        People who saw the revised map were more successful at identifying locations under hurricane or tropical storm watches. However, that improvement in recognizing watch areas did not lead to greater concern about the potential danger or stronger intentions to take safety precautions.

        The results also highlighted a persistent misunderstanding surrounding the familiar hurricane "cone of uncertainty." Many people incorrectly interpret the width of the cone as an indication of a storm's physical size, rather than understanding what the graphic is intended to communicate about the uncertainty in its projected track.

        "For years, research has shown that people often mistake a wider cone for a bigger storm," Ray said. "Our findings suggest that even with an updated graphic, that misconception can be hard to shake."

        Why Better Hurricane Warnings May Not Be Enough

        The NHC introduced its expanded forecast graphic in 2024 to help people recognize that hurricane dangers are not limited to communities along the coast. Flooding, tornadoes, and destructive winds can threaten areas well inland, making it important for residents beyond coastal evacuation zones to understand their potential exposure.

        By adding inland hurricane and tropical storm watches and warnings to the traditional forecast cone, the updated graphic provides a more complete picture of where dangerous conditions could develop.

        However, the study suggests that adding useful information to a forecast does not automatically make people more aware of their personal risk or more likely to respond appropriately.

        The researchers stressed that their findings do not call into question the quality of the NHC's weather predictions, which they consider highly accurate. Instead, the results underscore the difficulty of presenting complex meteorological information in a way that people can readily interpret and use when making safety decisions.

        "Our question wasn't whether the science is good," said co-author Patrick Merle, professor and director of the School of Communication. "The science is excellent. The question is how we communicate that science so people who aren't meteorologists understand the risk they're facing.

        If people find information credible but still don't understand it well enough to take protective action, then communication has fallen short. The goal is to empower people with information they can use to stay safe."

        The findings point to the importance of looking beyond a single forecast image when preparing for a hurricane. Although the cone can provide valuable information about a storm's expected path, it cannot communicate every hazard that a particular community might experience.

        The researchers recommend using hurricane forecast graphics alongside local weather predictions and detailed information about possible impacts. Understanding the specific threats to an area, as well as the protective steps residents should consider, can provide a more useful basis for making decisions.

        "Our findings are a reminder that one graphic can only tell you part of the story," Ray said. "Don't rely on the cone alone. Pair it with clear, local information about what impacts to expect and what actions to take. That gives you a fuller picture of what the storm could mean where you live and what to do next."

        The study received funding through an internal Florida State University SEED grant. The research was also informed by discussions with the NHC, which supplied the experimental forecast graphics evaluated by the team.

        Bradford Johnson, an assistant professor in the Department of Geography, contributed as a co-author.

        Materials provided by Florida State University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

        #2986. Morning My Son Sanctuary Tour From Hoi An: Going Early Changes Everything

        After spending most of December riding out the end of the rainy season in central Vietnam, I was finally waking up every morning to more and more beautiful days. The Hoi An, Vietnam weather in December is still pretty wet, but there’s so much emerging after the rainy season â lush greenery, the young rice paddies, and flowers beginning to bloom all around around Hoi An.

        This site participates in the Amazon Services LLC Associates Program and other affiliate programs and may earn from qualifying purchases.

        So I finally booked this sunrise My Son Sanctuary guided tour â an early-morning trip about an hour and fifteen minutes west of Hoi An, and similar in distance from Da Nang. The tour picked me up outside my hotel in Hoi An at 5:20 a.m., and after picking up two or three more groups â about ten of us total, from several different countries â we started the quiet drive west.

        It was a beautiful, calm ride ending in the sun peaking up over the young rice paddy fields, still waking from under their covers of fog and mist in the morning.

        In reality, I didnât really know what to expect as we arrived to My Son Sanctuary. I knew very little about Vietnam when I arrived, and admittedly, very little about the Vietnam War and the history surrounding it. Thatâs something Iâve been trying to remedy while Iâve been here â learning more about the past, and as a U.S. citizen, the role we played in it.

        After buying tickets to enter this Unesco world heritage site â about 150,000 Vietnamese dong, roughly six U.S. dollars at the time â we made our way into the park to the shuttle station, where small golf-cart-style vehicles carried visitors closer to the ruins.

        Our amazing (and kind of hilarious) tour guide, Lin, explained that the shuttle system was added in recent years to manage traffic as visitor numbers increased. Sheâd first been here more than twenty years ago, and she said that the contrast in tourism then versus now was striking, and that she is worried about the long-term.

        The shuttle dropped us near an open-air restaurant in the park where this specific guided tour included free coffee or tea before exploring â and lunch later! I loved the little pond filled with frogs belting out a surprisingly loud morning symphony. We chilled there for a bit to listen before heading toward the ruins.

        So My Son Sanctuary was an extremely central part of the Champa Kingdom, a civilization that lived in central Vietnam for centuries. Research shows that beginning around the 4th century, the Cham people built these temples as places of worship, largely dedicated to Hindu deities like Shiva. But you donât need to know much history to feel that this was once an important place.

        One of the details that stuck with me most was the writing â ancient Champa script etched directly into stone…and it’s freaking gorgeous! (Said the design nerd…)

        I think during these “touristy” type moments, I try to remember that these aren’t just âsome cool ruins,â or a place that lends to beautiful photos, but a place that once held real meaning for generations of people. There were serious spiritual and cultural activities that were an important part of life for those that lived here before they were ruins.

        The main architectural area of My Son Sanctuary is beautiful, but one thing that our guide explained very well is that much of what we see throughout the sanctuary has actually been rebuilt. Details like this is where the experience began to shift for me.

        I hadnât realized how involved this area was during the Vietnam war, even though I understood that the war mostly took place in central Vietnam.

        When our guide pointed out small pockmarks in the walls and asked us what we thought they were, the answer â bullet holes â stopped me in my tracks.

        She talked about how people around the country are still finding live bombs from the war, even now. She stood next to two casings from unexploded bombs â they were taller than she was.

        Seeing that in person was difficult and eye-opening. According to the Vietnamese government, an estimated 35 million landmines and 300,000 tons of unexploded ordinance still remain buried across the countryside decades after the war ended (source).

        Standing there, next to those bombs, that number and the literal impact the war had on this area and it’s people stopped being so abstract.

        We walked past massive indentations in the ground â twenty or thirty feet across â bomb craters left as they were. Our guide said she hoped theyâd never be filled in, so people would remember.

        Some of the bomb craters had been filled with water and turned into lily ponds. There’s something beautifully troubling about that contrast.

        One of the more fascinating details was learning how the original structures were built. Clay formed into precise blocks, coated with natural materials, then lit on fire (almost like a pottery kiln) as entire structures to melt the coating to create strength.

        In photos, you can clearly see the difference. The original blocks â hundreds of years old â often look cleaner and more intact than the newer reconstructed sections, which show more moss and discoloration. We also saw an area being rebuilt almost from scratch.

        It was a subtle reminder that many of the ruins we admire around the world â in Europe, in Vietnam, everywhere â are often reconstructions after centuries of destruction, often from wars.

        After finishing the loop through the ruins, we returned to the restaurant for lunch, which was also included in the cost of this specific My Son Sanctuary tour â which only cost about $25 USD by the way.         As we headed back toward the entrance at around 9am, the contrast between now and 7am was immediate. Hundreds of people were arriving and funneling towards the ruins.

        At one point as we left, we passed a group of what felt like a hundred school kids â laughing, yelling, and…well, being kids. Joyful, loud, and completely at odds with the calm weâd just experienced.

        Our guide mentioned that afternoon tours are rare â itâs simply too hot and humid. Most people visit earlier in the day, which only reinforced the value of going as early as possible.

        If youâre deciding whether to visit My Son Sanctuary or whether it’s worth it, my answer is a resounding yes â if you do it this way. Early. with a small group, and before the heat and the crowds.

        As I always say: get up early, get out of bed, explore first. Take your nap later. Itâs always worth it.

        We drove the hour and fifteen minutes back to Hoi An, and that wrapped up a day that left me with more questions than answers â the best kind of travel day.

        On the ride back, I spoke with a couple of Germans whoâd visited the DMZ (the demilitarized zone) and described it as one of the most emotional and enlightening tours they’d ever taking. Hopefully, thatâs next on my list.

        Being here has made one thing clear: understanding Vietnam â or most places in the world, really â means listening, learning, and sitting with its history. Especially when…and because…itâs uncomfortable.

        #2987. Build a Luge STEM Challenge

        Luge is one of our favourite Olympic events to watch. Luge involves one or two athletes sliding down an ice track at breathtaking speeds feet first on a small sled. It’s the fastest Winter Olympic Sport.

        Your mission in this activity is to build a mini luge track using a craft stick, coins and tape. It’s a great way to learn about gravity and friction.

        Does a heavier luge go faster down a ramp?

        Does the surface of a ramp affect the speed of a luge?

        Create a ramp using a stack of books and the card.

        Draw a start and finish line on the ramp.

        Choose what to investigate; remember to change only one variable at a time. Either use the same luge and change the ramp surface or use the same ramp surface and change the luge.

        Record the time taken for the luge to pass between the start and finish lines.

        Decide what to investigate and then repeat each condition three times. Calculate the average time for each luge or surface.

        The ramp can be as long or as short as you want; just remember to record only the time taken to travel between the start and finish lines.

        Before starting the investigation, predict which luge or which surface will be the fastest.

        If you investigated different ramp surfaces, you probably found that the smoothest surface was the fastest. This is because smooth surfaces have less friction between them, allowing them to move faster. The rougher the surface, the more friction acts between the surface and whatever is on top of it. Friction slows down moving objects.

        If you investigated different numbers of coins on the craft stick, the difference in speed is all due to gravity and momentum. When the craft stick is placed on the ramp, gravity pulls it down the slope. The more coins on the craft stick, the more mass the luge has. The greater the mass, the more momentum the luge creates as it moves down the ramp.

        If you have time, try both investigations and compare the results. A luge with more mass has more force to act against the friction, so it can maintain its speed better than a luge with less mass.

        Did you know? There is a strict weight limit for Olympic Luge sledges.

        #2990. Holafly Plans: The Best eSIM Plan for Long Term Travelers

        There are a lot of eSIM companies in the world. They all work a similar way: you download their app, pick your package, install the eSIM into your smartphone, and off you go. There’s many I like.

        But one I like more than the rest is Holafly and I’ve been using their eSIMs for years. I find their app to be easy to use and their rates really competitive.

        So when Holafly launched Holafly Plans, a single eSIM that works as an ongoing subscription across 160+ destinations, I was excited. It’s a great option for those who are looking for more data and flexibility than a traditional eSIM. (An eSIM is a digital SIM built directly into your phone’s hardware, so there’s no physical card to insert or swap. Instead of visiting a shop for a plastic SIM, you activate a plan by scanning a QR code or installing it through an app, and you’re connected within minutes. Because it’s software-based, a single phone can hold multiple eSIMs, letting you switch between a home number and a local or global travel plan without touching your device. I have like 10 installed. lol)

        In this post, I’m going to explain what the product is, how it’s different than a traditional eSIM, and who I think this option is for!

        Holafly Plans is a single global eSIM that covers more than 160 destinations on one ongoing subscription. You buy it once, install it once, and it just keeps working as you move from country to country. You don’t reswap SIMs, you don’t redownload anything, there’s no re-purchasing, and no hunting for a new QR code every time you land somewhere new.

        It automatically reconnects you when you land in a new place. Think of it as like a traditional mobile carrier – but a lot cheaper.

        You can buy it monthly or annually and it comes in two tiers: Light and Unlimited. There’s no annual contract and you can cancel any time.

        The Unlimited Plan includes no data caps and an unlimited hotspot so you can tether your laptop on the go. If you’re a digital nomad uploading video, doing video calls, or just someone who doesn’t want to think about data ever again, this is a great add-on. This tier also comes with a local phone number from the US, UK, or Canada, which lets you receive SMS messages.

        The Light Plan gives you 25GB of data a month, still includes a hotspot, and still covers the same 160+ destination coverage. For someone who mostly uses maps, messaging apps, and the occasional Instagram scroll, this package is probably the best option.

        Additionally, with both Holafly Plans, once your plan is active, you get their “Always On” feature which is 1GB of backup data every month, automatically for life, even if you eventually cancel your subscription entirely.

        Pricing varies a bit by region and currency and depending on how long you purchase, so check out their current rates on the Holafly Plans page. However, as an example, their unlimited plan is only $55 USD per month for 1 year, which is a lot cheaper than any US based carrier charges for their base option that includes unlimited 5G data overseas.  You can get monthly, quarterly, or yearly plans. Quarterly plans save approximately 10% while yearly plans save 15%.

        Classic Holafly eSIMs are built for a single trip. You pick a country or region (there are 200+ available), choose how many days of data you need, and it covers just that trip. If you’re flying to Portugal for two weeks, a classic eSIM is still the simpler, cheaper option. It’s a one-and-done purchase. If you only take a couple trips per year then this is still the best option.

        On the other hand, if you’re a digital nomad who is gone from your home country most of the year and moving between different countries all the time, then the Holafly Plans fly is the much better option. It’s a standing global subscription designed for people who are more or less always somewhere. Instead of buying a new eSIM every time your itinerary changes, you have one number, one connection, one bill that follows you across borders indefinitely. Holafly Plans costs less and gives you more.

        #2991. How to Keep Momentum Post-SSD

        You have been crushing our 8 week Spring Slim Down Challenge! We can't believe how fast this challenge went by! As the challenge comes to an end this Sunday May 4th, you might be wondering what to do next or how do I not lose this momentum?

        I have just the tips you need to stay on track and hit those goals! Plus, a BRAND NEW challenge, details at the end!!

        1. Set New Goals

        After completing the Spring Slim Down challenge, take some time to reflect on your achievements and set new goals for yourself. Whether it's improving your strength, increasing your endurance, or trying a new video in MOVE, having clear goals will help you stay motivated.

        2. Establish a Routine

        Creating a daily routine that includes time for exercise and healthy eating can help you stay consistent with your healthy habits. Make exercise a priority by scheduling it into your day, just like any other appointment.

        3. Accountability Groups

        Keep up with your Accountability group! Just because the challenge ended doesn't mean your group check ins have to! Having someone or a group to hold you accountable can make a big difference in staying on track. 

        4. Focus on Nutrition

        Remember that exercise is just one part of the equation. Paying attention to your nutrition is equally as important for maintaining a healthy lifestyle. Focus on eating whole, nutrient-dense foods to fuel your body and support your fitness goals.

        5. Celebrate Your Progress

        Don't forget to celebrate your achievements, no matter how small they may seem. Recognize the hard work and dedication you've put in during our Spring Slim Down challenge and continue to celebrate your progress as you move forward.

        #2992. Can plants defend coasts against storms?

        Thank you for visiting nature.com. You are using a browser version with limited support for CSS. To obtain the best experience, we recommend you use a more up to date browser (or turn off compatibility mode in Internet Explorer). In the meantime, to ensure continued support, we are displaying the site without styles and JavaScript.

        Rusty A. Feagin is in the Department of Ecology and Conservation Biology and the Department of Ocean Engineering, Texas A&M University, College Station, Texas 77845, USA.

        M. Luisa Martínez is at the Network of Functional Ecology, Institute of Ecology (INECOL), Xalapa 91073, Mexico.

        Plants and other organisms can reduce the erosion of coasts by winds and waves. The physical structures of sand-dune and wetland plants, seagrasses, oysters and macroalgae can slow the flow of water and wind, capture sediment and modify soil. Over time, these organisms can also build land, store greenhouse gases, provide habitats for other species and beautify beaches and wetlands1. In some cases, they can be used as a nature-based solution in place of engineered structures. But coastal engineers and planners are often hesitant to incorporate them into construction projects, because their ability to perform in extreme conditions is unknown. Writing in Nature, Willemsen et al.2 determine the limits of coastal protection that salt-marsh plants can provide.

        #2993. Summer Fitness Advice: Mark's 6 Basic Principles

        No doubt, summer arrives with its own rhythm, its own demands, and its own kind of freedom. Summer is an invitation to revitalize, rejuvenate, and redefine your fitness routine. As the mercury soars, the conventional gym walls might feel more like a prison cell than ever before. So, how about we shake things up a bit?

        Let's break away from the conventional and immerse ourselves in a summer fitness experience like no other. Brace yourself for an exhilarating journey to the best shape of your life.

        Here are my 6 basic principles for proper summer fitness.

        You can certainly train in the gym during summer. I do a lot of my training in the gym, indoors, throughout all seasons. But gym training isn’t unique to summer. Plus, it’s the default option for most people. I’m trying to get you to try something you haven’t tried before, to train in a way you can’t always train.

        Sure, if it’s 100 degrees out, you might want to do the gym. You might want to get some shade. But summer affords you the opportunity to train outside in most locales.

        Train barefoot in your background or a park. If you have gym equipment, simply take it outside and do what you normally do in the garage, only on the grass or dirt.

        Train in the forest. Climb trees, lift logs, throw boulders.

        Train at the beach or at the lake or river. Bring a kettlebell out there and get to work.

        The key to great summer fitness? Making it not just about the sweat and effort, but about the sheer joy of movement, the exhilaration of performance. If you make training fun, you actually look forward to it. You enjoy it in the moment, not just when it’s over.

        Seeking healthy pleasure is a cornerstone of being human and thriving as a human. Pleasure is how our brain reward systems get us to do things that are good for us. Exercise is no different.

        And just because it’s fun doesn’t mean you’re not going to be working hard or getting a great training effect from it. It means the workout is intrinsically valuable in the moment, and therefore more sustainable.

        The picture of this begins in your backyard, under the bright summer sun. In your hands, a kettlebell, that deceivingly simple yet incredibly effective piece of equipment. You swing it, lift it, press it, transforming each movement into an opportunity to build muscle, endurance, and strength. Each swing is met with a cool breeze, each lift underlined by the sun's rays bestowing upon you a golden glow and a healthy dose of vitamin D.

        Transition your training ground from your yard to the vast, blue expanse of a lake or an ocean. Envision yourself gracefully cruising on a stand-up paddle board, or perhaps navigating the waves in a kayak. It's not just a workout; it's an intimate connection with water, an element that has been entwined with our existence since evolutionary times. Comfort in water is a fitness staple, and summer is the ideal time to master it.

        On dry land, there's an entirely different adventure waiting for you. Picture yourself biking on winding trails or rollerblading down the coastline. Consider these not just as fitness tools, but as vessels of exploration. The goal isn't merely to burn calories but to soak in the surroundings, to integrate with the elements, to experience the freedom that summer brings.

        These tools— the humble kettlebell, your watercraft, and your chosen mode of land exploration— they are your partners in fitness, enabling you to tap into the unexplored power of outdoor training. What makes them special is that they aren't just exercising equipment; they are a part of your summer fitness story, enhancing the quality of your workout and your overall experience.

        Like what you’re reading? Share it so more people can find it!

        Instead of just using the rower at the gym, go kayaking down the river, through some sea caves, or across the lake (for time, if you wish to increase the intensity). Or, as I do, paddle down Miami canals, often meeting dolphins and manatees along the way.

        Instead of only walking on the treadmill, do a backpacking trek for a day or two. Carry your belongings up and down mountains, over boulders, through meadows and across rivers. You'll log far more steps than you ever would on the treadmill while getting an incredible sensory experience out of it.

        The gym is great. I use it myself all the time, no matter the season. But the point is to take your conventional gym training and use it to conquer the world and go on adventures.

        I’m not going to tell you what specific exercises to do. Deadlifts, squats, pull-ups, push-ups, presses, rows, swings, muscle ups, dips, lunges, step ups, hip thrusts… they’re all fantastic options. You know what you like to do. I am going to tell you to try being a bit more intuitive and “touchy feely” when you train. That’s what summer is: a season for intuition and feeling and spontaneity. Foster that summer spirit with your rep schemes.

        Say you’re doing push-ups, kettlebell swings, pull-ups, and squats. Or dips, trap bar deadlifts, rows, and lunges. A push, a pull, a row, and a knee flexion.Instead of hitting a set amount of reps, do the first exercise (push-ups) until you go just shy of failure. 2-3 reps left in the tank.

        Move on to the next one (kettlebell swings) and do the same. Feel that deep burn but keep a few reps in the tank. When you’re ready, return to the first exercise and do the same. Then do the same for the next one.

        When you’ve hit 3-4 sets of these, move on to the next two exercises and repeat the process.

        You’re learning how to feel when you’ve approached your limit. You’re getting the training effect for your muscles but you’re also training your intuition. That’s summer.

        Summer, for all its joys, brings with it an intense heat. But instead of letting it bog you down, let it fuel you. Embrace the warmth, revel in the sweat. Get filthy, dirty, dusty, muddy. Summer is your chance to build heat tolerance and improve your overall fitness level.

        It's also the time to use water to your advantage. Whether it's plunging through the waves at the beach, diving into a crystal clear alpine lake, swimming with (or against) the current in a river, or even doing laps and underwater pool workouts, the water is an entirely new playground for your fitness exploits.

        When the sun climbs the sky, don't hide indoors. Go outside and train in the oldest, biggest gym in the world. Soak up as much sunlight as you can, safely—don’t get burned, of course. It isn't just a pathway to a glowing tan, nor is it only a source of vitamin D, which is a crucial prohormone involved in muscle protein synthesis, bone density accrual, and hormonal health. It also increases nitric oxide production, which can improve the transmission of oxygen and important nutrients to your muscles and other tissues as well as enhance the “pump” you get when you lift weights.Furthermore, training in hot weather can add an additional stressor—the heat—that can enhance the training effect. However it is a stressor and you may need more recovery from an outdoor sun drenched workout, but you can also derive greater benefits.

        Just make sure that you stay hydrated. Add high quality salt to your water or make an electrolyte mix. I like to add a packet of LMNT to water plus collagen and creatine, then sip that before and during my workout. Hydration will improve not just your workouts but also your skin’s resistance to UV light.

        In essence, summer fitness is about embracing the inherent power of the season—the heat, sunlight, sand, water, and the calming energy of nature.

        Summer fitness is an exploration, an adventure. The sun-soaked season invites you to push beyond the typical, to break free from monotony and create a summer fitness routine that reflects the unique opportunities of the season. The world is your gym. Now go out there, embrace the heat, the sun, the water, and make your summer fitness journey an unforgettable one.

        How do you approach fitness in summer? What are your basic summer fitness principles?

        Mark’s Daily Apple is still under construction. My team and I are actively working to repost the archives. Check back regularly to find posts that are new to you and revisit old favorites. Looking for a specific post? Let us know here.

        You can help more people find us by spreading the word:

        * This blog reflects my personal views and opinions and isn’t intended as medical advice, but I hope it will be informative and inspiring as you pursue a healthy, fulfilling life.

        #2994. In Africa, a Growing Underground Solar Buildout

        Households and businesses are driving a solar boom in Africa, where rooftop arrays and other small systems account for the bulk of new solar installations. But this rapid buildout, which is driving solar additions to record levels, is largely unaccounted for in official statistics, analysts say.

        “The rapid spread of distributed solar in sub-Saharan Africa is an underreported success story,” write analysts at the energy think tank Ember and the African Tech Futures Lab. “However, since African countries are running blind to this change, they are not reflecting this rise in distributed solar in their plans.”

        Because most distributed solar systems are not tracked or regulated, most households cannot sell their surplus power back to the grid, analysts say. Ultimately, the lack of data on distributed solar could lead countries to underinvest in the batteries and grid infrastructure needed to store and distribute solar energy — or to overinvest in fossil fuels.

        Because official statistics are lacking, analysts used customs data to track the flow of solar equipment to Africa from China, the biggest manufacturer of solar panels globally. They found that Africa will add the equivalent of 17 gigawatts of solar capacity this year, more than enough to meet the continent’s average yearly growth in demand. In total, 36 countries will install record volumes of solar in 2026, while 19 will install more than twice as much solar as they did last year.

        In countries with rickety power grids, many people rely on diesel generators to cope with recurrent blackouts. But analysts say the Iran war has pushed up diesel prices, making solar a more attractive alternative. “Generating the same electricity from a diesel generator as from a solar panel would cost as much in diesel, every three months, as the solar panel itself,” they write. In almost every country in Africa, more than half of solar capacity installed since 2023 is distributed.

        “This transition is chaotic and disruptive, and far from the orderly model planned in national strategies,” said Joel Nana, research director at African Tech Futures Lab. “The choice now is whether these distributed assets remain a parallel power system that compensates for grid failure or are integrated in ways that unleash a wider range of benefits.”

        A First Among Major Nations, India Is Industrializing With Solar

        #2995. Astronomers uncover new secrets of the Universe’s first five minutes

        Scientists have measured the amount of helium produced during the Universe's first five minutes with unprecedented precision, offering a clearer picture of the conditions that existed shortly after the Big Bang. The achievement, made by an international research team that includes scientists from the University of Minnesota Twin Cities, represents a major advance in precision cosmology.

        The findings, presented in five papers published in The Astrophysical Journal, are based on 130 hours of observations using the Large Binocular Telescope. By analyzing distant galaxies, the researchers reduced the uncertainty in this fundamental measurement to just 0.5%, making it three times more precise than previous estimates.

        The improved accuracy provides scientists with a powerful new tool for investigating the origins of the Universe and testing the Standard Model of physics. Such fundamental tests have a long history of advancing scientific understanding and contributing to technological breakthroughs.

        "This is a physics experiment on a grand scale and one of the biggest findings in my entire 40-year career," said Evan Skillman, University of Minnesota College of Science and Engineering Distinguished Professor in the School of Physics and Astronomy. "It's a fundamental number that tells us specifically about the conditions of our Universe in its first five minutes. It has diagnostic power that speaks directly to the Standard Model of Physics."

        According to the Big Bang theory, the Universe began expanding from an extraordinarily hot, dense state approximately 13.8 billion years ago. Three major lines of evidence support this explanation: the continuing expansion of the Universe, the cosmic microwave background radiation, and the amounts of light elements such as helium and deuterium that formed during its earliest moments.

        Although cosmic expansion and the microwave background have been studied extensively, measurements of primordial helium have historically received less attention. Determining its original abundance more accurately allows researchers to investigate the physical conditions that prevailed when the Universe was only minutes old.

        To achieve their new level of precision, the team departed from the conventional method of estimating primordial helium. That approach involves observing large numbers of galaxies and extrapolating the results to determine how much helium existed before later generations of stars altered their chemical composition.

        Instead, the researchers selected 15 exceptionally pristine, small, distant galaxies. These galaxies have undergone relatively little chemical evolution, making them valuable cosmic "time capsules" that preserve clues about the Universe's early composition.

        Powerful Telescopes Deliver Record Precision

        The team used sophisticated spectrographs developed at Ohio State University to examine more than 10 helium spectral lines and 15 hydrogen lines simultaneously. Studying these signals together made it possible to identify and correct subtle sources of measurement error that earlier studies could largely overlook.

        Such small effects become especially important when researchers are trying to reduce uncertainty to less than 1%. Accounting for them was essential to achieving the new 0.5% precision.

        "The MODS spectrographs took 12 years to build from conception to first light on sky," said Richard Pogge, College of Arts and Sciences Distinguished Professor of Astronomy at Ohio State University. "This is the kind of project we designed them to do, and to see them deliver is enormously satisfying. It's not every day you can help build instruments that measure something fundamental about our Universe."

        Beyond refining estimates of the Universe's earliest chemical composition, the new measurement provides an important test of the Standard Model of physics. The amount of helium created shortly after the Big Bang depends on the physical conditions at that time, including the types of elementary particles present.

        Using their precise helium measurement, the researchers were able to calculate the number of neutrino families in the early Universe. Neutrinos are extremely light subatomic particles, and their behavior plays an important role in scientists' understanding of fundamental physics.

        The results therefore do more than strengthen existing evidence for the Big Bang. They also offer a more precise way to check whether established physical theories accurately describe the Universe during its first few minutes.

        "We promised a half-percent uncertainty in our proposal, and we got there," added Skillman. "In the unpredictable world of science, that doesn't happen very often."

        The international collaboration brought together scientists from the University of Minnesota Twin Cities, Ohio State University, Gonzaga University, Northwestern University, The University of Texas at Austin, Indiana University, University of California Santa Cruz, University of Illinois, Universidad Nacional Autónoma de México, and TRIUMF.

        Materials provided by University of Minnesota. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

        #2998. Easy Winter OLYMPICS STEM Challenges

        The 2026 Winter Olympics start tomorrow, bringing athletes from around the world together to compete for a coveted gold medal! Here at Science Sparks, we’re looking forward to watching gravity-defying spins, jumps and twists and super speedy slides.

        Winter Olympic sports are full of cool science, the games are a bit like a huge, frozen physics lab. Have you ever wondered why a curling stone seems to slide so easily, or how a ski jumper flies through the air for so long? The games are a great opportunity to watch science in action!

        I’ve created a few easy winter Olympic science activities to bring the thrill of the mountain to your living room.

        Curling is a team sport where two teams slide granite stones across a very long strip of ice towards a target. Curling stones are heavy, weighing around 20kg.

        Each player has a brush to move the stones and special shoes. One curling shoe has grips, and the other is slippery, allowing them to slide the stone as they play while still gripping the ice when needed. Brushes are used to warm the ice where players want the stone to move. This is called sweeping. Sweeping reduces friction between the stone and ice.

        Curling ice isn’t smooth like the ice used for skating and ice hockey, it has tiny bumps called pebbles that are created by spraying water on the ice. The pebbles reduce the surface area of the ice that contacts the stone, allowing it to slide more easily rather than just sticking to the ice.

        This activity uses a paper plate with weights on it to be a curling stone and tests different materials on the underside to reduce friction between the plate and the floor.

        Test different materials on the underside of a paper plate to find which makes the plate (the curling stone) move the fastest.

        Try rough and smooth materials to investigate which reduces friction between the plate and the floor the most.

        Tape a few coins to the top of a paper plate. This is just to make it harder to push.

        Mark a start line on a flat, open surface.

        Gently push the plate and record how far it goes.

        Choose one of your test materials and use it to cover the bottom of a paper plate.

        Gently push the plate from the start line again. Try to use the same force as with the first plate and record how far the plate travels.

        When players sweep the ice, they generate heat that melts the top of the ice pebbles, reducing friction between the ice and the stone and making the stone move faster!

        Build a bobsleigh track using kitchan foil and a marble or small ball as the sledge. Record how long the marble takes to complete the track.

        Change the gradient (steepness) at the start of the track and record the time taken again.

        Place a coin on one end of a craft stick. This is your luge. Record how long the luge takes to go down a ramp, with the coin at the front.

        Move the coin to the middle of the stick and record the time again.

        Move the coin to the back of the craft stick and record the time taken to go down the ramp again.

        Which coin position makes the luge travel the fastest?

        Wrap one ice cube in insulating materials such as bubble wrap, black paper or kitchen foil.

        Leave another ice cube on a plate uncovered,

        Check the ice cubes every 2 minutes and record which one melts first.

        Last Updated on February 6, 2026 by Emma Vanstone

        #2999. The Best Hotels Across Thailand That Deliver Island Luxury, City Elegance, and Jungle Seclusion

        When it comes to the world's most luxurious places to stay, Thailand hotels punch well above their weight. In Bangkok, glossy city hotels rival those found in New York and London, and some of the country's landmark addresses are tucked away in beach towns. Chiva-Som is regularly voted as the best destination spa in the world, while Aman, Banyan Tree, and Six Senses properties are dotted throughout the best islands in Thailand. There are plenty of boutique stays and under-the-radar bolt-holes to be found too, where friendly service and traditional cooking will leave you feeling like you've gained a glimpse into life as a local. For a shortlist of our favorites, we rounded up the best hotels in Thailand below.

        The Thai capital has no shortage of swanky beds to book, but it's only once in a blue moon that a hotel with such star power as the Capella Bangkok comes around. Opened in 2020 on a garden-clad estate along the Chao Phraya River, this low-rise pile of blonde wood and creamy marble entered the scene with such Bangkok firsts as travertine hot tubs on suite balconies, and villas pitched up right on the waterfront. Côte, the hotel's Mediterranean-tinged fine-dining spot by Argentine chef Mauro Colagreco, earned a Michelin star within a year after opening, while those in the know flock to the boudoir-like Stella Bar after dark to sip on drinks inspired by some of Bangkok's most storied districts.

        Compared with many of its rivals, this Bangkok stalwart doesn’t have a location that’s wildly convenient: The postcard sights need a boat transfer and then a taxi ride. But for the legions of loyal fans of this monochrome masterpiece by hotel guru Bill Bensley, that’s the point. Pitched up on a lush riverside plot in the hushed Dusit district, The Siam feels like a portal to a bygone Bangkok. Even though it opened over a decade ago, it’s easy to imagine it as a plush playground for the city’s erstwhile big guns: the midcentury movers and shakers whose heirlooms—vintage Pan Am posters, tattered travel trunks, and chipped ceramics—adorn the marble-floored hallways. The Thai capital’s five-star hotel scene gets plusher every year, but this timeless beauty remains in a league of its own.

        Aman Nai Lert Bangkok is not a place one loves then leaves. It stays with you. For nearly a year, over and over, I’ve wished to be back in its ninth-floor infinity pool, delicate shadows from a massive sampong tree canopy dancing on the salt water as a wispy breeze fluttered in my hair. I can’t count how often I’ve begun salivating when I've thought of the sweet-and-salty moo ping pork skewers and limitless bowls of melt-in-your-mouth mango I devoured at breakfast. And I crave deeply the energy-releasing tap-tap-tap of the hand-carved teak tools used in the hotel spa’s Nud Tok Sen massage.

        These are not only happy memories; they are all connected to the rich storytelling of this urban resort built amid a seven-acre bubble of lushness that was the early-20th-century teak retreat of visionary Thai entrepreneur Phraya Bhakdinorasreth, known as Nai Lert. His descendants’ hotel and private members club is intimately tethered to its place down to the littlest detail. Glamorously artful and thoughtful in both aesthetics and hospitality, the bolt-hole is perhaps Bangkok’s most personal, chockablock with intimate references in a setting best described as elysian. Being a guest here feels like being woven into the fabric of its history too. —Kathryn Romeyn

        This riverside grande dame offers old-world, illustrious glamour and pomp without feeling too fussy. With a clientele that includes mesmerizing dignitaries, celebrities, and noted authors (not to mention everyday travelers) and benefits like exceptional service, a storied lobby, a fleet of BMW sedans, and a tropical, riverside location, it’s no wonder that the Mandarin Oriental is so renowned. Upon arrival, you’ll nearly strain your neck trying to take in all the splendor. The lobby is a palace of lattice woodwork, cascading florals, giant birdcage-style chandeliers, and printed sofas. Expect views of the city’s burbling river, best enjoyed from the emerald-green swimming pool, tiki cocktail in hand. The Mandarin is where one could broker an international deal, find a strapping future spouse, or simply shut out the rest of the world. Dress to impress and pack a designer bag (or at least an exceptional fake), because this crowd looks like it was pulled straight from the White Lotus. —Jenny Adams

        Teak pillars and Lanna roof trims are the norm in Northern Thailand, but Raya Heritage in Chiang Mai city plays a different game. Designer Vichada “Dao” Sitakalin built this riverside retreat and filled it with crafts—ceramics, basketry, hand-dyed indigo textiles—sourced from village artisans and embedded in the decor of every cream-colored suite. There’s a boutique selling the same pieces, a tea lounge serving local brews, and a restaurant, Khu Khao, that draws on regional influences with dishes such as Burmese mohinga and Yunnanese rice salad. Add a riverfront pool and herb-heavy spa treatments, and you’ve got Chiang Mai’s most rooted—and gorgeous—stay.

        There's a small-town feel to this retreat, which opened in April 2023 in Chiang Mai's leafy Umong district. At its heart, four antique Thai dwellings, with their hand-carved gabled roofs and teakwood frameworks still intact, harbor the hotel's main facilities, from a four-bedroom villa with breezy balconies and a private pool to a boutique selling local crafts. Pitched between the bamboo-fringed communal pool and a stilted meditation lounge, the wellness center is a highlight, whether you opt for the week-long mindfulness retreats or a quick reflexology fix-up.

        The restaurant, taking over one of the villas, is just as wellness-minded: days here start with ginger-turmeric elixirs, superfood bowls, and tofu-stuffed Thai lettuce wraps, but the rest of the menu is far from hair-shirted: options feature Northern Thai noodles and Lanna-style riffs on comfort classics—think tacos with pulled wild boar belly, and fish and chips from snakehead fish with spicy khao soi ranch dressing. With bright interiors accented with local textiles and wickerwork, the 44 rooms and duplex suites hit the sweet spot between rootsy Thai and comfortably modern.

        Treetop pavilions, private residences, and pool villas sit on 31 acres of manicured gardens and a rice paddy within the Mae Rim Valley. Come sundown, the rice farmers parade through the fields singing and celebrating the end of a working day. Two buffalo bathe daily in the lake, which acts as a hub for activities from sunrise yoga to pottery making, Thai boxing, tennis, and textile dyeing. For guests who prefer to spend their stay wallowing in the sunshine, there are two infinity pools that stretch out into the paddies. It's a lush and serene scene from every angle.

        Private residence guests can spend the day without seeing a soul, in grand gated homes built for families who enjoy hiding away in pure luxury—private pools, a kitchen space to hire private catering, and a wraparound terrace for alfresco dining and entertaining. Eating here is a highlight, especially breakfast, which draws on color and cuisine from all walks of the world, from Chinese dumplings to English breakfast, and coffee orders taken by the friendliest of staff. —Sophie Knight

        Chiva-Som is the undisputed queen of the Thai wellness scene—when it arrived on the scene 27 years ago, spa breaks in Thailand weren't even a thing. There have been plenty of pretenders since, but none have yet managed to usurp the original, with its life-boosting combination of cutting-edge fitness (new machinery includes Iron Man–like Vision Bodysuits), medical diagnostics (blood work, gene testing), traditional Asian healing therapies, and hyper-intuitive staff. There are 16 different retreats to choose from—stress and pain management, fitness boost, immune resilience, senior health—each of are minutely personalized to meet all of your mental, physical, and emotional needs. This is the place to change the habits of a lifetime. —Adrianne Pielou

        Some two hours into the three-hour drive northward from Bangkok, the landscape starts changing. Concrete sprawl morphs into Khao Yai’s undulating hillscape, bedecked in thick jungles and fruit orchards, and just off the area’s main road that snakes past pastiche European-looking country clubs and patchworks of farmland, the InterContinental Khao Yai emerges from behind a hedgerow of towering trees.

        Dotted around the lush estate, you’ll see railway-themed buildings and train cars—which is the exact theme Bangkok-based hotel designer Bill Bensley was going for. The lobby is built like an old-timey ticketing booth, while suites are decked with upcycled vintage railway carriages and draw inspiration from different Asian railway journeys. Also set up in railway carts are two restaurants and one bar (head to Poirot for mushroom tarts and boeuf bourguignon) and a spa specializing in Thai and aromatherapy massage. Needless to say, this imaginative stay is a hit with little ones, but even grown-ups will appreciate the whimsical respite from busy Bangkok.

        On the secluded northwestern tip of Koh Samui’s glassy Laem Yai Bay, Bill Bensley, architect of the Four Seasons Tented Camp Golden Triangle in Chiang Rai, has scattered 60 gorgeous hillside villas and 10 private residence rentals among the coconut trees. The villas all come with expansive Gulf of Thailand views (including from the tub), as well as decks with big plunge pools, shaded daybeds, and a stocked wine fridge.

        The resort has a small, effectively private strip of white-sand beach by the main infinity pool, as well as sea-facing daybeds at the laid-back beach bar, where the attentive staff whips up an array of cocktails. The adjacent dining pavilion, Pla Pla (meaning “many fish” and named for its fresh-catch menu), is a romantic dinner spot, and the restaurant KOH Thai Kitchen & Bar highlights indigenous dishes like tom kati talay, a coconut soup studded with seafood, tamarind, and kaffir lime leaves. Massages inside one of the spa’s tree house–inspired treatment rooms knead away any last trace of real-world stress. —Hannah Summers

        The original outpost of the globe-spanning Aman empire, this Phuket property was initially intended as a holiday home for Indonesian hotel guru Adrian Zecha. Eventually, he expanded the blueprint into a small boutique resort that opened in 1988. In the following decades, Amanpuri became a hush-hush haven for monarchs and supermodels, with yearly touch-ups and regular renovations constantly making it look brand-new. The hotel’s Thai temple–like pavilions have since been imitated by resorts all over Southeast Asia, but the ones surrounded by lush gardens and coconut palms here, accented with timeworn Buddhist antiques and fresh orchids, still feel like the real deal.

        During a recent project, the pavilions’ honey-hued interiors shed their low-slung ceilings to reveal the high-pitched teak wood beams that were hidden behind, while the old bathtubs in the mirror-clad bathrooms were replaced with newer models. The Holistic Wellness Centre, which offers everything from crystal healing sessions to high-tech IV infusions and medical check-ups, is one of Thailand’s very best. Despite the worldwide acclaim, the resort remains blissfully serene even during the high season, although scoring the best canvas-covered beach cabanas can turn into a game of musical chairs. Ask one of the hosts to try to reserve your favorite one.

        Privacy is assured at this indulgent spot, which rolls down a tropical hillside onto a quiet corner of upmarket Layan Beach in the northwest of Phuket. Forty frangipani-scented acres and 6,562 feet of Andaman Sea coast are dedicated to just 39 peaked-roof villas and 30 palatial private residences—all with private pools and panoramic sea views—spaced far enough apart that guests never have to see each other. You can eat at Pru, the only restaurant in Phuket with a Michelin star and the only one in Thailand with a Michelin Green star, or join one of the buzziest Sunday Champagne brunches on the island. You'll also want to fit in a visit to the temple-like Jara spa to try some integrative Thai therapy, combining massage with wooden hammers and chisels—and perhaps a little bit of magic.

        It’s only a short speedboat ride from jam-packed Phuket, but Anantara’s perch on the eastern tip of palm-tufted Koh Yao Yai island feels like a wholly different slice of southern Thailand. By the time you arrive at the resort’s jetty, which juts out from a jumble of tropical trees, any hint of Phuket’s notorious beachside bustle has long disappeared into the blue horizon. Wedged between jungle-cloaked hills and a cream-colored beach, the resort unfurls like a small town (yet gargantuan by Koh Yao Yai island standards) of multi-storied buildings with wavy façades and roofs covered in grass smooth as a golf course. European families on school break mix with Thai weekenders at the breezy restaurants and the blob-shaped pool, which curves around wooden islets dotted with sun loungers, swirling pathways, and lush patches of tropical greens. It’s a far cry from the snug, small resorts that have occupied these islands so far—but for a few days of beachy, margaritas-by-the-pool self-indulgence, this one’s hard to beat.

        Anantara Mai Khao Phuket is real fly-and-flop territory—touch down at the airport, and you’ll be at the gates of the hotel in just 20 minutes and sipping iced coffee by your pool shortly after that. Compared to other luxe hotels on the island—many of which can be a two-hour drive away in heavy traffic—this alone could be a reason to stay. The rooms are also huge. All 83 of the standalone structures are private villas with extensive outdoor terraces, huge pools, bedrooms, and bathrooms. Thoughtful design touches include sliding glass doors separating your living space and pool, and a huge stone outdoor bathtub (big enough for two) separated from the pool by a glass panel, allowing you to slip between hot soaks and cooling dips.

        The real star of a stay here is a meal at the Tree House, a Japanese omakase built into the branches of an ancient banyan tree. You can choose to dine at a table, gazing out over the gardens, but the more exciting seat is at the counter, watching the chefs prep ingredients that have recently jetted in from Japan. There's a spa and a kids club, as well as a babysitting service if parents are in need of a night off. —H.S.

        What are the best places to visit in Thailand?

        If you want to dive into street food markets, restaurants, bars, galleries, and museums, then Bangkok is the place to be. The capital city is also a transport hub, with AirAsia operating affordable flights across Thailand—including to Chiang Mai, Udon Thani, Trang, Phuket, and Krabi—and other Asian destinations. For more, see our ultimate two-week itinerary for Thailand.

        As with many far-flung tropical destinations, the “best time to visit Thailand” is an entirely subjective question. Thailand’s islands vary in climate, and there are deals to be had at certain times of the year—even at the best hotels in Thailand—as most travelers aim to avoid the rainy season. Generally speaking, the best time to visit Thailand is from December to March—though, as with any tropical destination, a short and ridiculously torrential burst of rain is unpredictably possible, even on the sunniest of days. For more advice, see our guide to the best time to visit Thailand.

When choosing hotels, our editors consider properties across price points that offer an authentic and insider experience of a destination, keeping design, location, service, and sustainability credentials top of mind.
